- Every control a test or an agent drives. Every label is translated, so no visible label is a stable locator.
- A value a test reads (`heading`, `cell`, `badge`), and every state and message (`status`, `message`, `error`).

What does not: decorative elements, layout wrappers, a `div` that scopes nothing, an icon inside a marked control. A control that is not a test target is declared so with a coverage-ignore marker and its reason (`// ui-contract-ignore: <reason>`), never given a made-up anchor.

**Icon-only controls** also carry an `aria-label` from a translation key, so a screen reader and an agent reading the accessibility tree can name them.

**Repeated items.** One static anchor marks every entry, and the entity's id goes into `data-id`: `device.list.session-item` with `data-id` = the device session. An entry whose entity differs from the others, or changes while it stays listed, carries a row state; an entry in its normal state carries none (`current` for this device, `revoked` for one signed out, nothing for the others).

**Composites and the shell's anchors.** A composite takes one `testId` and derives its parts. A composite that can be mounted on its own is not a singleton: the shell's instance owns a reserved anchor, and every other mount passes its own `testId`. `OptionSelect` is the shell's language select, which owns the reserved `app-shell.locale-select`, and the organisation page's choice of organisation, which passes `organisation.page.current-select`. Each `ConfirmDialog` is mounted by its view with an anchor of that view's screen (`user.profile.sign-out-dialog`, `device.everywhere.dialog`). A feature never declares or renders a reserved anchor.

**Locating an element**, in this order, stopping at the first that works:

1. `getByTestId('<anchor>')`.
2. `getByRole('<role>')` without a name, inside the container found by its anchor.
3. `getByLabel(...)`.
4. `getByPlaceholder(...)`.

Text is used only for text the test itself entered (a note it wrote, an email it invited). Never a CSS class or the shape of the DOM; a selector over the contract's attributes is fine: `[data-testid="organisation.notes.item"][data-id="..."]`. When a test has to fall back to steps 3 or 4, the element needs an anchor: ask for one.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.kind`](#screen-anchors-name-kind), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataId`](#screen-anchors-name-dataid).

### Readiness and container states

<a id="rule-readiness"></a>
Wait for `app-shell.status` to reach the state `ready` (`[data-testid="app-shell.status"][data-state="ready"]`): the frame and the screen are rendered. Its other states, and what each means, are the vocabulary's table "App states". Then wait for the screen's container to leave `loading`: a `list`, a `section` or a page `status` carries one of the vocabulary's "Container states".

On a screen's `states`: The container states of the screen, each a row of the vocabulary's Container states table with what is visible in it: `loading`, `ready`, `empty` and the like say when the data of a list, a section or a page status has arrived; a screen's `states` say what is visible in each, never what each means.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states), [`screen.states`](#screen-states), [`manifest.screens.states`](#manifest-screens-states).

### Notices and ranked messages

<a id="rule-notices"></a>
`app-shell.notice-message` shows one notice at a time, the latest, and it outlives the page that raised it (a notice an action raises just before its flow navigates shows on the page it navigates to). Find it by its anchor and its code together, right after the action that raises it.

A message whose codes more than one source may raise before it shows (an effect of the app and a `show` of a flow on its way there) says which one it shows in its structure: `ranked: true`, with its `codes` in rank order. Of the codes raised before it shows, it shows the first, and the others go with it. A code raised waits until the message shows or a flow clears it: a `clear` of the message, in any flow, takes away every code raised before it. `contract:check` takes `ranked` on a message of two codes or more, each listed once, and `contract:diff` lists each ranked message with its order.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.ranked`](#screen-anchors-name-ranked), [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes).

### A message whose codes are ranked

<a id="rule-ranked-message"></a>
`ranked: true` on a message: A message whose codes are in rank order: of the codes raised before it shows, it shows the first, and the others go with it; a clear of it, in any flow, takes away every code raised before it. A code raised waits until the message shows or a flow clears it. For a message whose codes more than one source may raise before it shows (an effect of the app and a `show` of a flow on its way there): it says which one it shows in its structure, with its `codes` in rank order. Only a message of two codes or more, each listed once: `contract:check` refuses it elsewhere. The manifest carries `ranked` in `ids`, and `contract:diff` lists each ranked message with its order, `<anchor> ranks <code> > <code>`, a new order as the old line removed and the new one added, which its codes compared as a set do not show.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.ranked`](#screen-anchors-name-ranked), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.ranked`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-ranked).

### Routes: the bindings of a screen

<a id="rule-route-bindings"></a>
A screen's `bindings` are its routes in the router's format: `path` (static segments, `$param`, `{-$param}` for an optional one, `$` for a splat), `guard` (`guest`: only a visitor; `authenticated`: only a signed-in account), the `roles` it serves, `params` and `search` as flat JSON Schemas, `history` (`push` or `replace`) and `retain`. The frame (`app-shell`, `frame: true`) has no route: it is around every screen.

Every binding `path` is a TanStack Router path in the router's own syntax, `params` are its path parameters and `search` its search parameters, and a route is a state address: the same path with the same search reaches the same state after a reload. The application's router builds and matches every binding (`apps/web/src/platform/routes-router.spec.ts`, a parameter's first example its sample), and the module routes equal the bindings (`routes-contract.spec.ts` beside it); the same folder's `routes-examples.spec.ts` holds every example link and the route tree to the real router. `contract:check` wants every path to parse in the router's syntax, every example of a route parameter to match that parameter, and a `$param` without one to have a value its schema accepts.

A screen is named by the path of its first binding: `/me` is `me.yaml`, `/org/$org` is `org/$org.yaml`, the frame without a binding is `__root.yaml`.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings`](#screen-bindings), [`screen.bindings.path`](#screen-bindings-path), [`screen.bindings.history`](#screen-bindings-history), [`screen.bindings.retain`](#screen-bindings-retain), [`screen.frame`](#screen-frame).

### Roles and who a route serves

<a id="rule-roles"></a>
A role is one of the terms file's `roles` (see the rule `terms`). A device holds a set of roles: `guest` when nobody is signed in on it, else `identity` and, beside it, every other role that applies to the signed-in account (its role in the current organisation, a role of the platform, any role a later entry adds: the roles other than the two). `guest` and `identity` are the two roles of which a device holds exactly one; every other role is held beside `identity`, never alone. The contract names the roles; how a device learns the ones beside `identity` is the app's. `GUEST`, `IDENTITY`, `heldRoles` and `admits` of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors` read them for the app and the checks alike: `heldRoles(signedIn, others)` is the set a device holds (`guest` alone, else `identity` and each of `others` but `guest`), and `admits(roles, held)` says whether a binding's or an anchor's `roles` admit a device that holds one of them.

A binding's `roles` are the roles it serves: the router serves it only to an account that holds at least one of them. An account that holds none gets the screen of the binding at `/$` (the not-found screen) at the same address, as an address no route takes does: the URL and the history entry stay as they are, and nothing on the page, the tab's title included, says there is a route there. The router decides in this order: the binding's `guard` (`authenticated` sends a guest to `/sign-in`, `guest` sends a signed-in account to `/me`), then its `roles`, and only then reads the search, so a search the screen would refuse shows an account its roles miss the not-found screen, never the screen's refusal. A route that must not show itself to a guest leaves its `guard` out and names no `guest`: a guest gets not-found, as any account the roles miss does. The binding at `/$` serves every account: its `roles` name `guest` and `identity`.

The roles of a device change while a screen is open: an account signs in or out, another organisation becomes the current one, a role is granted or taken back. The router decides again when the app learns of it. A screen whose binding no longer serves the account is replaced by the not-found screen and its state is dropped, as when another screen opens (a read of its in flight is dropped with it); a not-found screen at an address whose binding now serves the account is replaced by that screen, which opens as a screen opens (a list with a platform source reads it, see the rule `list-reads`). On a screen that stays, the roles the device holds now decide whether an anchor with `roles` may show, whatever state its flow is in, a final one included, and with no event of the flow: one they no longer admit is hidden, and one they now admit shows where everything else that decides it lets it (its state renders it, the anchors above it, `own`, `with` and `without`, its data). An account signing in in another tab hides a link shown to a guest alone, while the flow of the screen stays in its state. An anchor its state does not render stays away whatever the roles (see the rule `anchor-roles`). An app that learns of a change late decides again when it learns; no check sees the interval.

`contract:check` holds who a route serves: no binding's `guard` sends away an account its `roles` name (`authenticated` with `guest`, or `guest` with any other role); an anchor's `roles` are among those its screen's bindings serve (the frame aside); a link to a route whose roles name neither `guest` nor `identity` is shown to no device the route refuses (see the rule `anchor-roles`); and the binding at `/$` names `guest` and `identity`. `contract:diff` lists who each route serves as `<path> guard <guard>` and `<path> serves <role>`, a route added whole with every line.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.roles`](#screen-bindings-roles), [`screen.bindings.guard`](#screen-bindings-guard), [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles).

### The example link of a route

<a id="rule-route-example"></a>
A parameter of `params` or `search` may carry `examples`: fake values it accepts, of its type (`key: { type: string, examples: [ thg_01hzx9k2 ] }`); `contract:check` holds each one to the parameter's schema. `bun run contract:routes` (`ui-contract routes [--json]`) prints the routes of the screen files in four sections: the table (path, screen, guard, roles, history, params, search, file), one example link per binding, where each route is reached from (the anchors linking to it with `binding.route`, the screens whose flow navigates to it, the activity states at that address) and the tree as the router holds it, `__root (app-shell)` with each route under it, a route under the binding whose path is the longest proper prefix of its own on whole segments.

The example link is built from the binding:

- the path: each `$param` at its parameter's first example, else its `default`, else its first `enum` value, else a value built within its bounds and a `pattern` of a literal prefix and at most one repeated class (`^thg_[a-z0-9]+$` gives `thg_a`), the first of them not empty that the parameter's schema accepts; a parameter none of whose values can be built needs an example, and `contract:check` says so (`a path segment cannot be empty` for a schema that allows only the empty string); a `{-$param}` at its first example, or its segment left out without one; an empty example of a path parameter is a problem; a splat `$` left out with its segment (no parameter can be named `_splat`, so it takes no example); each value through `encodeURIComponent`;
- the search: every key in its declared order at its `default`, else its first `enum` value, else its first example, a key with none left out; written as TanStack Router's default `stringifySearch` writes it: a string bare unless it parses as JSON, then quoted (`"123"` is `%22123%22`), a number or a boolean as JSON, the whole form-encoded the way `URLSearchParams` encodes it (a space is `+`).

So a binding `/things/$thing` whose `thing` has `examples: [ thg_01hzx9k2 ]` and whose `search` has `view` (default `read`) and `page` (default `1`) has the example `/things/thg_01hzx9k2?view=read&page=1`. The example shows every search key with its default on purpose; the app's own links leave out a value equal to its default, and both forms reach the same state. The manifest carries the link as `example` on each binding, and `apps/web/src/platform/routes-examples.spec.ts` holds every example to the real router (the path it builds, the search string it writes, and the route, parameters and search it matches for the link) and the router's route tree to the tree of the screen folder.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.params.properties.<name>.examples`](#screen-bindings-params-properties-name-examples), [`screen.bindings.search.properties.<name>.examples`](#screen-bindings-search-properties-name-examples).

### The anchors a test starts from

<a id="rule-screen-entry"></a>
A screen's `entry` lists the anchors a test starts from on the screen. Some of them are alternatives by state, so a test waits for the one its state shows, never for all of them: `user.profile` lists `user.profile.heading`, `user.profile.form` and `user.profile.save-button` once the profile is read (`ready`); until then, and when the account has no profile, `user.profile.status` shows instead (`loading`, `empty`). A screen whose data arrives after the page lists its status and its list the same way: the status while the data is read (`loading`), the list once it is (`ready`).

A screen's `states` say what is visible in each container state, each state a row of the vocabulary's Container states table; the `wireframe` is the default state in Playwright's aria snapshot grammar.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.entry`](#screen-entry), [`screen.states`](#screen-states), [`screen.wireframe`](#screen-wireframe).

### The project's terms, its roles, and the roles and routes in code

<a id="rule-terms"></a>
`contract/domain/terms.yaml` holds the project's language: the bounded contexts the contract speaks in (`contexts`, one while the product is one), the platform's terms (`terms`: account, organisation, membership, ...) and the roles a device holds (`roles`). Each term and each role has its `meaning`, a written description that keeps 200 characters; its `synonyms`, the words the contract's texts and the emitted constants never use for it; and its `storage`, the name storage gives it where storage names it otherwise (organisation is the table `tenant`), so no applied migration is renamed to match. The file is the business layer's, like every file of `contract/domain`, and every contract writes it.

The roles are there alone: the loader makes them the contract's roles, which every check reads (`vocabulary.roles`), and a vocabulary that still lists a Roles section is a problem (`termsProblems`). A contract without the file is a problem too; one of an older ref that lists its roles in the vocabulary still loads, so `contract:diff` reads it. In the file a name is a term or a role, never both; a synonym is a word no term or role is named, forbidden for one of them only and listed once; and the terms belong to at least one context.

No forbidden synonym stands in the contract's texts or in what the engine emits from them (`synonymsProblems`): every written description (`readDescriptions`, the terms' own meanings included), every behaviour text of the platform (an outcome line, a code's condition, an invariant's rule), every platform action's and invariant's name, every row of the vocabulary's tables (its key and its meaning), every model action's name and the confirmation's code it derives, every activity's id, title and state names, the prose of every scenario (its title, its Where line, its sentence, its steps and expected results, read past their bracket references), every screen's id, every binding's route path and a further binding's key (`<id>@<path>`), every role's name, the name and the enum of every platform value and of every field of the model, and every entity's name, which every key of its fields carries (what `CONTRACT_VALUES` holds), and every declared anchor's name and every part it derives (what `FIELD_FACTS` and the code carry); a longer name built on a shorter one is named only for what it says beyond it. A synonym is read as whole words in any case, its words apart by anything that is not a letter or a digit, its last word also with a plural `s` or `es`, never as a part of a word; each is named once per text, at its first place, with the term or the role to write instead. Code and storage keep their names: the check reads the contract alone.

Code and tests name a role through `CONTRACT_ROLES` and a route through `CONTRACT_ROUTES`, which `ui-contract emit terms` writes (see the rule `emitted-modules`), never by hand. The ESLint rule `workspace/contract-terms` of `@workspace/eslint-config` (its factory `contractTerms`) holds them to it: a string is a role or a route written by hand where the code expects one, which TypeScript says (the type the code declares at the string, the other side of a comparison, the discriminant of a `switch`, the keys an object is expected to have) is made of string literals that are all roles, or all routes. Where no other kind of path is written (the e2e tests, the web app's screens) a string, a part of a template or a regular expression that names a route by its value is refused too; the gateway's own API paths can be the same strings, so the gateway and the web app's adapters to it are held by type alone. An invariant's number is named through `CONTRACT_INVARIANTS` (`CONTRACT_INVARIANTS['failed-sign-ins'].limit`), and the rule refuses one written by hand where the code expects one: a property of an object typed by an invariant's numbers (whole or picked), a variable or an argument whose annotation names one of them (`typeof DEVICE_LIMIT` included), or the other side of a comparison whose value comes from one, followed through member reads, constants, parameters, destructured bindings, imports and re-exports, the literal read through an `as`, a `satisfies` or a `!`. Numbers are common words of code too, so only the module's own declaration says the code expects one: a literal type of the code's own says nothing, and a bound compared with a counter of the code's own (`i < 10`) is out of reach. A role SurrealQL writes (a schema's assert, a query, a fixture) is held to `CONTRACT_ROLES` by `bun run lint:surql`, part of `bun run check`: one the terms file does not name fails, so a renamed role fails there until SurrealQL follows.

Keys it concerns: [`terms.contexts`](#terms-contexts), [`terms.terms`](#terms-terms), [`terms.roles`](#terms-roles).

### A flow: its states and regions

<a id="rule-flow-states"></a>
A screen's `flow` is an XState v5 machine config, in the subset `createMachine` takes as written: `contract:check` runs it in `setup({ guards, actions, actors }).createMachine(flow)`, with a stub per guard, effect and invoked actor, and an app that builds its own machine for the screen holds it to the flow with the engine's comparison (`@workspace/ui-contract/machines`), as it holds its router to the bindings. The route owns the addressable state, what a link or a reload restores; the machine owns the transient one (signing in, confirming, failed). A screen's transient state comes from its flow run as its machine, never from a component's own flags.

- `initial` and `states`. A state has a `description`, `type: final` where the flow ends (the app has left the screen, or stops), `entry` and `exit` effects, `on` and `invoke`.
- A state of the root may be a parallel state, `type: parallel`, for the independent areas of a screen (the organisation page's create form, new note, notes' items, invitation and members): its `states` are its regions, two or more, all active at once. A region has a `description`, an `initial` and its `states`, plain states that never end, carry no `meta.address` (the address is the screen's state; their `meta` holds `renders` alone) and hold no states of their own. The parallel state has a `description`, `on`, `entry`, `exit`, `meta.address` and `meta.renders`, never an `initial` or an `invoke`; its own `on` applies whichever state each region is in. Entering it starts every region at its `initial`; leaving it leaves every region.
- The flow's `id` is a one-segment name the flow declares, which the app gives its machine and an id target `#<id>.<state>` names (one segment, as XState splits a target on `.`); absent, the app names the machine by the screen id.

Nothing else of XState is in the subset: no `context`, `after`, `always`, states nested deeper than a parallel state's regions, history states, targets by `.child` or by an id from anywhere but a region's state, or actions named by a string. A state of a region is the area's own: where the app keeps one per item (each note's edit form), the region follows the item acted on last and says so. The flow's states are the machine's; the screen's `states` stay the container states.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow`](#screen-flow), [`screen.flow.id`](#screen-flow-id), [`screen.flow.initial`](#screen-flow-initial), [`screen.flow.states`](#screen-flow-states), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.type`](#screen-flow-states-name-type), [`manifest.screens.flow.id`](#manifest-screens-flow-id).

### Events, targets and guards

<a id="rule-flow-events"></a>
`on` maps an event to one transition, or to a list of guarded ones in XState's order, the one without a guard last. An event is the user acting on a declared anchor (a derived one through its base's `derived`; typing, for a field), a platform event of `platform.yaml` `events` (`platform.opened`), or a data event `model.<entity>.<field>`: the stored value of a field that an anchor of the screen shows changed, here or on another device (`model.profile.name`). A transition has a `target` (absent: the machine stays where it is), a `guard`, `actions` and a `description`.

XState resolves a target from the parent of its state: at the root it is a state of the flow, or a state of a region as `<parallel>.<region>.<state>` (`viewing.create-form.open`: that region starts there, the others at their `initial`); in a region it is a state of that region, or a state of the root by the machine's id, `#<flow id>.<state>` (`#organisation.viewing`), which leaves the parallel state and enters that state afresh, the parallel state itself with every region at its `initial`. Within a parallel state an event belongs to one area, one region or the state's own `on`, never two; the same event may appear in several states of one region.

A guard is a camelCase name that the root `meta.guards` declares with what it holds: a sentence (`holdsUnsentChanges: this device holds changes the server has not received`), or the failure codes it takes, `{ codes: [ <code>, ... ] }`, when it tells the failures of an action apart: it holds when the action failed with one of them. A guard by failure code guards only branches of an invoke's `onError` (a region written `by` its button included), and each of its codes is one the invoked action fails with, listed once; the sign-in screen may write `emailNotVerified: { codes: [ email-not-verified ] }` for the branch of `signing-in` that leads to `unverified`. A bare `show` on such a branch shows the guard's codes (see the rule `failure-show`). The four guards of a password pair are the loader's: it derives them from the confirmation's `confirms` (see the rule `password-pair`). An activity's guards are sentences. The app implements every guard.

A control whose anchor is an event of the flow (a button, a select, a switch) is enabled exactly while a state the machine is in handles that event (`snapshot.can` in XState), and disabled otherwise: the profile's Save is disabled while it saves because neither `saving-name` nor `saving-bio` handles it. The app reads it from its machine and keeps no flag of its own. A password input's `visibility-button` is never an event of a flow, so the machine does not enable it: it is always enabled.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on`](#screen-flow-states-name-on), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.target`](#screen-flow-states-name-on-name-target), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.guard`](#screen-flow-states-name-on-name-guard), [`screen.flow.meta.guards`](#screen-flow-meta-guards), [`screen.flow.meta.guards.<name>.codes`](#screen-flow-meta-guards-name-codes).

### Which controls the machine enables

<a id="rule-enabling"></a>
A control of `ENABLED_BY_STATE_KINDS` (of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors`: a button, a tab, a select, a checkbox, a radio, a switch) whose anchor is an event is enabled exactly while a state the machine is in handles that event, as XState's `snapshot.can` answers it: a transition whose guard fails does not handle it. A text field and a link are never disabled by state: typing goes on while a request runs.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on`](#screen-flow-states-name-on).

### The four effects of a flow

<a id="rule-flow-effects"></a>
The effects, in `actions`, `entry` and `exit`, are four: `{ type: show, params: { anchor, codes } }`, the anchor shows one of the codes it carries; `{ type: clear, params: { anchor } }`, an anchor that carries codes shows none until one is shown again, and a field is emptied; `{ type: read, params: { anchor } }`, a list of the flow's own screen whose `source` is a platform action reads that action again (see the rule `read-after-an-action`); and `{ type: navigate, params: { to, search } }`, the app moves to that binding. The app implements all four.

The codes come first: a `clear` of an anchor that carries codes, whatever its kind and its screen, a field included, means it shows none until one is shown again. A field that carries none (an input, a textarea, a select, a checkbox, a radio or a switch, a `secret` one included) is emptied, and only on the flow's own screen: it returns to its empty or initial value and holds no input any more, as if nothing was typed; a form empties its passwords this way once the action that takes them succeeds. A `clear` never names a `visibility-button`, nor a button, a list, a link or any other anchor unless it carries codes.

Each value of a navigate's `search` is a search key of the binding it moves to, given a literal (`$id` for a minted id) or `{ anchor: <field> }`: the value a field of the flow's own screen holds when the app moves (an input, a textarea, a select, a checkbox, a radio or a switch; never a button, a list, a `visibility-button` or a `secret` field, whose value would stay in the address, the browser's history and the logs). A link that carries the email typed so far to the page that asks for a password reset: `navigate: { to: /forgot-password, search: { email: { anchor: user.sign-in.email-input } } }`.

A value may also be `{ item: <item> }`: the `data-id` of the entry of that `item` or `row` whose control the user acted on, so that the selection is in the address and a reload or a copied link reaches the same entry. Only the event of a control (a button, a link, a tab or a field, never a cell or a sibling entry) in the item's part (`<part>.<name>`, like the item), rendered in each entry, carries it, so the navigate sits in the actions of that event's transition, never in an `entry`, an `exit` or the outcome of an invoke, where no entry was acted on. The item is an entry of a list, a table or a section of the flow's own screen, declared under it or beside it in its part, and declares its `dataId`; the search key it fills takes a string. A row that opens the entity it shows: `navigate: { to: /<path>, search: { id: { item: <screen>.list.<entity>-item } } }` on `<screen>.list.open-link`. A navigate that sets a search key a paged list of the screen it moves to reads says `page: 1` (see the rule `paged-list`).

The scope of each effect differs. A `show` names a declared anchor, of any screen (the frame's toast and notice included), and codes that anchor carries. A `clear` names a declared anchor that carries codes, of any kind and any screen, which then shows none, or else a field of the flow's own screen, never a `visibility-button`. A `read` names a list, a section or a table of the flow's own screen whose `source` is a platform action. A `navigate` names the path of a binding of some screen and search keys of that binding, and sits on a transition, never in an `entry` or an `exit`, moving once (see the rule `leave-questions`). An address, `meta.address`, is a binding of the flow's own screen (its first one when the path is left out) with search parameters of that binding.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.show`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-show), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.clear`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-clear), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.read`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-read), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.navigate`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-navigate), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.navigate.search`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-navigate-search).

### What empties a field

<a id="rule-clear-keeps"></a>
Only a `clear` of the flow empties a field while its draft lives (see the rule `flow-effects`): a field the flow never clears keeps what was typed in every state, and a state that stops rendering it hides it without emptying it. A draft lives with what it belongs to and starts afresh, from the stored value or empty, when that ends: with another organisation (the page's flow says every area starts afresh), another key of the screen's address (see the rule `address-key`), or an entry's edit form closing because another entry is edited or deleted (the flow says what was typed is dropped).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.clear`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-clear).

### An invoke and a state that has an address

<a id="rule-flow-invoke"></a>
An `invoke` runs a model or a platform action while the machine is in its state: `src` names it as a binding does, `input` maps each parameter as a binding's `params` do: to the anchor whose value it takes, a constant `{ value: ... }`, or a search parameter of the route `{ search: <name> }`, and `onDone` and `onError` say what follows its success and its failure. Every `input` key is a parameter of the action; a parameter left out is the implementation's to compute (a minted `$id`, the `author`). An invoke may say `read: false`: its action changes nothing a list of the screen shows, so nothing reads after it (the copy of an invitation link); one branch of its `onDone` or `onError` may say `read: false` instead, where another branch reads: nothing reads after that outcome (see the rule `read-after-an-action`). An event that runs an action is a transition into such a state, named for what it does (`signing-in`, `saving-name`, `deleting`), or `running` in the region of a button.

A state that a route reaches says so in `meta.address`: its `path`, a binding of the screen, and its `search`. The route owns the addressable state, what a link or a reload restores; the machine owns the transient one (signing in, confirming, failed). One transient state is exempt and no flow holds it: whether a password input shows what it holds, which the input keeps itself and its `visibility-button` toggles (see the rule `visibility-button`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.input`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-input), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.read`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-read), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.meta.address`](#screen-flow-states-name-meta-address).

### The shorter forms of a flow

<a id="rule-flow-shorter-forms"></a>
A screen file may write its flow shorter, and leave out what the contract says elsewhere (see the rules of what a screen leaves out); the loader expands it into the config above before anything reads it, so the checks, the walk in XState, the manifest and an app's machine see the full config, and `bun run contract:flow <screen>` prints it. The shorter forms are four:

- A transition written as its target alone, as XState itself allows: `onDone: idle` is `onDone: { target: idle }`.
- An effect written by its name: `show: <anchor>`, the anchor shows one of the codes it carries, in its order (in the `onError` of an invoke, one of the codes the failure of its action shows, narrowed by a guard by failure code; see the rule `failure-show`); `show: { <anchor>: [codes] }`, one of these; `clear: <anchor>`; `read: <anchor>`; `navigate: <path>` or `navigate: { to, search }`.
- A region written by its pattern, a shape that two regions or more of the screens share, named by what the area does. A shape that one region has is written in full until a second one shares it. `draft`: a control that follows a stored value or holds what was typed. In `following` the control `input` shows the stored value as it changes, here or on another device; typing moves it to `edited`, where it keeps what was typed until that is the stored value again, by typing it back or by the data event `stored`, both under `guard`, and goes back to `following`.
- A region written by its button, `by: <button>`: the region of a button bound to one action (see the rule `button-region`) with what the screen decides of it, its `description`, `onDone` and `onError`, and the `toast: false` or `read: false` it says of its action, which the loader puts on the region's invoke; a branch of its `onDone` or `onError` that says `read: false` keeps it. An outcome left out is the derived one; a transition without a target goes back to `idle`.

The states of a pattern and of a button's region are the machine's, named by the loader (`idle`, `running`, `following`, `edited`), and a scenario or a test reads them as it reads any state. What the shorter forms leave out is mechanics a tester or a user cannot tell apart; a `description` belongs to a decision (where a success leads, what a failure shows, what restarts what), not to every state. The states of a region written by its pattern or by its button list nothing in `meta.renders`.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.pattern`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-pattern), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.by`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-by), [`screen.flow`](#screen-flow).

### The area of a button across its request

<a id="rule-area-request"></a>
The area of a button bound to one action is the button's part, and the area's error is `<part>.error`, when the screen declares it. The button's region, derived or written `by: <button>` (see the rule `button-region`), keeps that error while its action runs: entering `running` clears nothing, unlike a request that writes no `onError` (see the rule `request-outcome`). A refusal shown on the error replaces it with its own code. From version 5 a success clears it: every branch of the region's `onDone` that neither shows nor clears `<part>.error` starts with `clear: <part>.error`, derived, so `console.account.name` forgets a refused rename's error once a rename goes through. A screen file never writes that clear in such a branch: it is a problem at the clear (derived: a success clears its area's error; leave it out). A clear of another anchor stays the screen's, and so does a region written in full, whose outcomes say what they clear; an error a region shows outside its part (the members' removal of `organisation` shows on `organisation.invite.error`) is not its area's, and its success clears it only where the region writes so. Before version 5 a success clears only what the region writes.

A list, a section or a table an outcome reads again (`read: <anchor>`) keeps its state and its entries while that read runs, and the answer replaces them, a failure included: `loading` shows only while nothing of its current search is read yet, at its first read or a read for another search, organisation or account (see the rule `list-reads`), never during the read after an action. In the area, a field keeps what was typed through the action and the read after it, until a `clear` of the flow empties it; a field that starts with what a read gives (`console.account.name.name-input`, the name as read) shows it only until something is typed into it, and no later read replaces what was typed.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.by`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-by), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.onDone`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-ondone).

### A flow run in XState, and an app held to it

<a id="rule-flow-run"></a>
`contract:check` holds the flow to the contract and runs it in XState (the engine's `machines/walk`): a pure walk with `initialTransition` and `transition` reaches every state, a parallel state's regions each on its own with the others at their `initial`, and takes every transition and every guarded branch to its target with its effects. An app that builds its own machine for a screen holds it to the flow with the engine's comparison (`@workspace/ui-contract/machines`; the web app's `apps/web/src/platform/machines.spec.ts`): the machine has the config of its flow, what each state renders included (on a branch, the flow less what it added and the machine has not built yet), an invoke's or a branch's `read: false` aside, which is a promise the checks read and nothing a machine runs. The comparison reads the contract at the branch's merge base with `main`, and at the commit the branch is at, in ahead mode (see the rule `ahead-mode`).

Carry the flow into `setup({ guards, actions, actors }).createMachine(flow)` unchanged: implement every guard it names, the four effects and an actor per `invoke.src`. `projected(config)` is what the comparison reads of a machine config (initial state, states, final states, what each state renders as a set, regions, events with their targets and guards, invoke sources, the contract's effects, `read` among them; the app's own actions and an invoke's or a branch's `read: false` left out), and `beyondTheSubset(config)` what a machine does that the flow subset has no counterpart for.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow`](#screen-flow).

### What a state renders

<a id="rule-renders"></a>
An anchor that only some states render, those states list in `meta.renders`: a plain state of the root, a parallel state or a state of a region. An anchor no state of the flow lists, neither itself nor one it is under, is rendered in every state. An anchor listed, itself or through one it is under, is rendered only where it or that one is listed: in a plain state of the root that lists it or that one, and in a state of a region that lists it or that one, or whose parallel state does. A name is an anchor its screen declares, listed once; a derived part is rendered with its base, so a state lists the base (`user.sign-in.password-input`, never its `visibility-button`). A listed anchor also renders the anchors declared under it (`<anchor>.<rest>`, as a scenario's codes and states speak of them): `user.sign-in.password-rule-list` brings the six items of the composite with it, so a state lists the list alone, never the list and one of its items in one list, and an anchor declared under a listed one is rendered only where its container is, or where a state lists it itself.

A plain state of the root or a state of a region that handles an event on an anchor only some states render renders that anchor (for an event on a derived part or on an anchor declared under another, through the anchor it is under), since a control the user acts on is on the page; a state may render a control it does not handle, which is then disabled. The events of a parallel state's own `on` are not held to this, and neither are the effects: a `show` or a `clear` may name an anchor its state does not render. The states of a region written by its pattern or by its button, or derived by the loader, list nothing: an area that renders something in some of its states only is written in full. For the modes of the sign-in screen (the rest of its flow left out):

```yaml
flow:
  initial: sign-in
  states:
    sign-in:
      meta:
        renders: [ user.sign-in.forgot-link ]
    sign-up:
      meta:
        renders: [ user.sign-in.password-rule-list, user.sign-in.password-confirmation-input, user.sign-in.name-input ]
    check-mail:
      meta:
        renders: [ user.sign-in.forgot-link, user.sign-in.resend-button ]
    # signing-in as sign-in; password-weak, passwords-differ and signing-up as sign-up;
    # unverified and resending as check-mail
```

The form, the email and password inputs and the submit and toggle buttons are listed by no state, so every state renders them; the forgot link is rendered only in the states that list it, and the password rule list with its items only in the states of the mode sign-up. `passwords-differ` handles `user.sign-in.password-confirmation-input`; without its list, `contract:check` would say `"user.sign-in.password-confirmation-input" is handled here but not rendered: only the states that list it in meta.renders render it`. A state that runs an action lists what stays on the page while it runs: `signing-up` lists the rule list, the confirmation and the name input, or they would go while the account is created. `contract:diff` lists each entry as a line `<state path> renders <anchor>`.

Rendered means visible to the user, as far as the state decides: an anchor a state renders is on the page and seen unless a condition keeps it hidden: its own, one of an anchor it is under, or one of its base if it is a derived part (`roles` or `own`, an entry's data, `with` and `without`); one it does not render is not seen: absent from the page, or kept in it hidden (`hidden`, `display: none` or `visibility: hidden`, as the page helper's `visible` reads it), as a form that keeps what was typed into it while it is closed. The app reads what a state renders from its machine, whose states carry the flow's `meta.renders`, through the engine's `renderedAt` and `isRendered` (`@workspace/ui-contract/anchors`; `isListed` says whether an anchor is rendered only where listed, and `listedBy(state)` is how all of them read one state's list), never from a flag of its own, as it reads which controls are enabled; the engine's comparison holds those lists to the flow's, and an e2e run holds the page to them in every settled state a test passes through (see the rule `settled-state`).

`contract:check` holds `meta.renders`: it lists anchors its own screen declares (a derived part's base, never the part), each once and never beside an anchor it is declared under, and a plain state of the root or a state of a region renders every anchor only some states render that it handles (one declared under a listed anchor, through that anchor).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.meta.renders`](#screen-flow-states-name-meta-renders), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.states.<name>.meta.renders`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-states-name-meta-renders), [`manifest.screens.flow.states.<name>.meta.renders`](#manifest-screens-flow-states-name-meta-renders).

### Anchors several states render, named once

<a id="rule-render-sets"></a>
A flow may name a list of anchors several states render (`flow.meta.renders: { <set>: [anchors] }`); a state that lists the set's name in its `meta.renders` renders those anchors, as if it listed them; the loader writes them in. A set serves two states or more.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.meta.renders`](#screen-flow-meta-renders).

### When an element shows beside others: with and without

<a id="rule-with-without"></a>
An element that shows only beside other elements of its screen, or only away from them, says so in its structure, never in its description: `with` lists the anchors each of which renders while it does, `without` those none of which renders while it does. An entry is an anchor of the element's own screen (the frame's, on the frame), or `{ <anchor>: [codes] }`, that anchor while it shows one of these codes. The element renders only while every `with` entry renders and no `without` entry does: that says when it may show, and its state (see the rule `renders`) and its data (its roles, its owner, its value, its entries) still decide whether it does. For the frame's reminder to install the app:

```yaml
anchors:
  app-shell.install-later-button:
    kind: button
    with: [ app-shell.install-message ]
  app-shell.install-button:
    kind: button
    with: [ { app-shell.install-message: [ install-available ] } ]
```

An anchor rendered in each entry of a list, in the part of its `item` or `row` (`<part>.<name>`, like the entry) or declared under it (see the rule `entries-of-a-list`), names anchors of the same entry and means the same entry: `device.list.revoke-button` with `without: [ device.list.current-badge, device.list.revoked-cell ]` shows on every entry but the one of this device and those signed out already. An anchor outside the entries may name one of them, and then means any entry.

`contract:check` refuses an entry that names the element itself; an anchor of another screen (a screen's element never names the frame's, nor the frame's a screen's) or one its screen does not declare; a code the named anchor does not carry, as loaded (the codes the loader derives included); an anchor named twice, in one list or in both; an anchor that names the element back, directly or through others, a cycle, which is said on one of its anchors only; and, under an entry, an anchor outside that entry. The manifest carries `with` and `without` in `ids`.

An e2e run holds them in every settled state of the element's screen (see the rule `settled-state`), unless the element is rendered in each entry of a list, which the report does not tell apart: it is not visible while a `with` entry does not render or a `without` entry does. A state that lists the element, or one it is under, in `meta.renders` requires it on the page only while its entries say it shows; while it is not visible, neither the parts derived from it nor the anchors nested under it are required. A control that no state lists, neither itself nor one it is under, which nothing besides its state decides, is visible once each of its `with` entries renders, one at least, and no `without` entry does; any other element shows by its own data too (a cell its value, a list its entries, a message its code), and an element with no `with` entry has nothing on the page that says the area holding it is shown. A code entry reads the `data-code` the report carries; where the report carries none, it is judged only when its anchor is not visible, and then it does not render.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.with`](#screen-anchors-name-with), [`screen.anchors.<name>.without`](#screen-anchors-name-without).

### Who sees an anchor: its roles

<a id="rule-anchor-roles"></a>
`roles`: The roles that see the element, among those its screen's bindings serve; absent: every role of the screen, as far as the anchors above it and a link's derived roles admit (below).

A role is one of the contract's roles (see the rule `terms`), and a device holds a set of them (see the rule `roles`).

Every restriction above an anchor admits a device by the roles it holds together: its own `roles`, those of each anchor it is declared under and its screen's (the frame aside), so one restriction naming only roles the route serves is enough, wherever it sits on the chain, and a link to a route whose roles name neither `guest` nor `identity` is shown to no device the route refuses.

`contract:check` wants an anchor's roles among those its screen's bindings serve (the frame aside); the manifest carries them, and `contract:diff` lists each role an anchor both sides hold is shown to as `<anchor> <role>`.

A state of a flow may list such an anchor in `meta.renders` like any other, and the state still bounds where it may be visible: an anchor a state does not render is never visible there, and the e2e run's settled check reports it. Where the state does render it, data (the account's roles) decides whether it shows, so the settled check does not require it to be on the page or visible there.

A link bound to one route takes the roles of that route when it admits fewer devices than the screen serves (see the rule `link-roles`). With no `roles` on it, written or derived, and none on an anchor above it, it is shown to every role its screen serves, whoever is signed in.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.roles`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-roles), [`manifest.ids.<name>.roles`](#manifest-ids-name-roles).

### When an element shows on the entries a member owns: own

<a id="rule-own-entries"></a>
`own`: Shown to a member only on the entries the member owns: the anchor sits in an entry of a list, and shows on the entries the member owns alone.

A state of a flow may list it in `meta.renders` like any other anchor, and the state still bounds where it may be visible: a state that does not render it never shows it, which the e2e run's settled check reports. Where the state does render it, data (the owner of each entry) decides whether it shows, so the settled check does not require it to be on the page or visible there, as it does not for an anchor with `roles`, an entry (`dataId`) or an anchor nested under one of these.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.own`](#screen-anchors-name-own), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.own`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-own), [`manifest.ids.<name>.own`](#manifest-ids-name-own).

### A list with no entries

<a id="rule-empty-list"></a>
A list with no entries is not rendered, so it is not rendered before its first read answers, unless it carries a data state (`loading`, `empty`) that shows it waiting or empty.

An e2e run holds it in every settled state (see the rule `settled-state`): a visible `list` none of whose entries is visible, an `item` or a `row` it contains (declared under it or beside it in its part, see the rule `entries-of-a-list`), is reported unless its `states` include `loading` or `empty`; a list that declares no entry is not judged. A state that lists such a list, any but a composite's, whose items are always there, does not require it on the page, nor its parts or what is nested under it while it is not visible.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states).

### A cell with no value

<a id="rule-empty-cell"></a>
A cell with no value is not rendered.

So an e2e run never requires a cell to be visible, and a `with` entry that renders does not require the cell that shows with it (see the rule `with-without`).

### Where an error shows each code

<a id="rule-error-placement"></a>
An error shows each code it carries in its own element: a code its `fields` maps to a field shows under that field and marks it invalid; a code mapped to `form`, or not mapped, shows beside the controls of its part (`<part>.*`), or alone where the part has none.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.fields`](#screen-anchors-name-fields), [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes).

### When a list reads its source

<a id="rule-list-reads"></a>
A `source` says where the entries of a list, a section or a table come from, and with it when they are read; no other kind carries one. `{ platform: <action> }`: the entries are the answer of that platform action, which the anchor reads each time it starts to render after it did not: the screen opening, a state entered that renders it (`meta.renders`) after one that did not, the account's role becoming one its `roles` admit; and, while it renders, each time the organisation or account it reads for becomes another, or a search parameter its source takes does (a `{ search: <name> }` of its `params`, and `page` and `size` when it is paged). The list shows none of the entries of the earlier search once the search is another, and a read still in flight when what it reads for becomes another is dropped: its answer is shown nowhere. Entering a state after another that rendered it too reads nothing by itself (a change of scope or search, or a `read` of the flow, still reads), so an anchor no state lists, neither itself nor one it is under, rendered in every state, is read only at those moments.

Every other read is said by the flow, with the effect `read: <anchor>` (see the rule `read-after-an-action`): a read after an action that changed what the list shows, never prose in a description. The `changes` of an action names the sources whose answer it changes, so it tells which lists of a screen that runs it show something else once it succeeds, and the flow holds a read of each of them after that success. `{ entity: <entity>, order, direction }`: the entries are the stored entries of that entity of the model, which the anchor follows as they change, here or on another device; such a list is never read.

A platform source's `params` give the action's parameters their values: each a search parameter of the route, `{ search: <name> }`, one every route of the screen declares, or a constant, `{ value: ... }`; never a field, since what a list shows is a state of the address and what is typed is not, so a search box moves the route with a navigate and the list, while it renders, reads the new search. A parameter left out is the implementation's to compute, as in a binding. Each param of a platform source is a parameter of its action taking a constant or a search parameter every route of the screen declares, never `page` or `size`.

`searchKeysOf(source)` of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors` computes the search parameters a source reads; the list rereads its source when one of them changes while it renders: those its params take, then `page` and `size` when it pages. `contract:diff` lists a list that reads a platform action as `<anchor> reads platform.<action>`, then each param by name (`q=search:q`, `kind="all"`), then `paged` when it pages: a list moved to another search or to paging shows as its old line removed and its new one added, and a list added whole shows its line.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.source`](#screen-anchors-name-source), [`screen.anchors.<name>.source.platform`](#screen-anchors-name-source-platform), [`screen.anchors.<name>.source.params`](#screen-anchors-name-source-params), [`screen.anchors.<name>.source.entity`](#screen-anchors-name-source-entity), [`platform.actions.<name>.changes`](#platform-actions-name-changes).

### The order of a list

<a id="rule-list-order"></a>
A list shows its entries in the order its source gives them, never sorted again by the screen: an entity source by its `order` and `direction`, a platform source as its action answers, its `result` naming the order where the contract holds one. A description that names an order names the one the source gives.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.source`](#screen-anchors-name-source), [`screen.anchors.<name>.source.entity`](#screen-anchors-name-source-entity).

### The read after an action, and its opt-out

<a id="rule-read-after-an-action"></a>
`{ type: read, params: { anchor } }`, written `read: <anchor>`, names a list, a section or a table the flow's own screen declares whose `source` is a platform action (see the rule `list-reads`): it reads that action again and shows its entries once the read is in, and the app may take them from the answer of the action that just ran, when that action returns them (a sign-out of another device answers with the account's devices when they could be read after it, else without them). A `read` never names an anchor of another screen, one with no `source`, nor one whose source is an entity of the model, which follows the stored data as it changes and is never read.

A read after an action is written on the outcome of its invoke (`onDone`, or an `onError` after which the list may have changed); an action that changes nothing a list of the screen shows says so on its invoke, `read: false`, and an invoke that says it and reads in one of its outcomes is refused. An action after which one outcome reads and another does not says it on that branch of its `onDone` or `onError`, `read: false` beside the branch's `guard` (the role change that demotes the account itself, after which the members list goes): only a branch of an invoke's outcome may say it, never the transition of an event; a branch that says it and reads itself is refused, once per read, and so is one under an invoke that says `read: false` already. XState ignores the key in both places, the checks and `contract:diff` read it, and the machine comparison leaves it out. `contract:diff` lists an invoke's `read: false` after its input and a branch's after its guard (`viewing.member-roles.changing onDone #1 -> idle [ownDemotion] read: false`).

The read after a success is mandatory where the action may change what a list shows. An invoke holds each list, section or table of its own screen whose `source` is a platform action when its `input` takes an entry of it (an `item` or a `row` it contains, as a role change takes the members list's item), when a value of its `input` equals one its source's `params` take (the removal of an account takes `{ search: id }`, which the account's section reads its action with), or when its action's `changes` names that source (`organisations.invite` changes `organisations.invitations`, which a sent-invitations list reads); a region written `by` its button is held as its invoke. Every branch of its `onDone`, a single transition being one, then reads each list it holds, `read: <list>`, or says `read: false`: the check refuses each list a branch leaves unread, at that branch, saying why the invoke holds it. A failure holds no read, so an `onError` reads where it says, and an invoke that says `read: false` holds nothing.

Every action, of the platform or of the model, writes its `changes`: the reads whose answer, for the organisation, the account and the params the action ran with, differs once it succeeds, each a platform action, never the action itself, that a list, a section or a table of some screen takes as its `source`, written once, `[]` for none. A switch of organisation or account, the first read's, is never named, nor what another account reads for itself. `contract:diff` lists the reads each action of the model comes to change or stops changing, as `model action lines added in <entity>.<action>: changes <read>`.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry.read`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry-read), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.read`](#screen-flow-states-name-on-name-read), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.read`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-read), [`platform.actions.<name>.changes`](#platform-actions-name-changes), [`manifest.screens.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.read`](#manifest-screens-flow-states-name-on-name-read), [`model.x-perch.actions.<name>.changes`](#model-x-perch-actions-name-changes), [`manifest.platform.actions.<name>.changes`](#manifest-platform-actions-name-changes).

### The key of an address

<a id="rule-address-key"></a>
A search parameter that is the screen's key (`x-perch.key: <code>`, a code of an action the screen runs with `{ search: <name> }` whose parameter refers to a value): a missing value, or one that breaks the value its action takes, is that action's failure `<code>`, shown as the flow shows that failure, without asking the platform; another value of it starts the screen afresh: the flow at its initial state, its fields empty, its errors cleared. A route has one key, and a key has no `default`. The search parameter of a mail's link is the key of its screen, derived (see the rule `mail-link-search`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.search.properties.<name>.x-perch.key`](#screen-bindings-search-properties-name-x-perch-key).

### A paged list

<a id="rule-paged-list"></a>
`paged: true` makes the list a page of the action's entries. The action says it pages and which page sizes it answers, `paged: { sizes: [ 10, 25, 50 ], default: 25 }` in `platform.yaml` (each size once, the smallest first, the default one of them): it takes the number parameters `page`, from 1, and `size`, one of the sizes, and answers the entries of that page, in the order its `result` says, with the number of entries the other parameters find in all. The page and the size are the route's search parameters `page`, an integer with a minimum of 1 and a default of 1, and `size`, an integer whose `enum` is the action's sizes and whose default is its default, which every route of the screen declares; the source takes them from there, so no `params` names them, and a screen pages one list. A new search starts at page 1: a navigate that sets a search parameter the list reads, or `size`, says `page: 1`.

A paged list derives four parts, which its `derived` lists and a screen file never writes (the loader lists them in the `derived` of the list whose source says `paged: true`). Its name has at most 3 segments (`console.users.list`, never `a.b.c.list`): its size options add two (`<list>.size-select.option`), and an anchor has 5 at most, so the check refuses a longer paged list.

- `<list>.range`, once the read is in: the numbers of the first and the last entry of the page and the number of entries in all (`51-100 of 312`), carried as `data-from`, `data-to` and `data-total`, with `0` and `0` where the page holds no entry (`0 of 312`: a page past the last).
- `<list>.previous-link`: a link to the same route, the same search and `page` one less, on every page but the first.
- `<list>.next-link`: the same with `page` one more, on every page that does not hold the last entry.
- `<list>.size-select`: a select of the page size, shown wherever the list is, whose options (`<list>.size-select.option`) carry the action's sizes as `data-value` (the values of the action, never rows of the vocabulary's Choice values) and are the controls of `size`, which therefore writes no `x-perch` (the check derives them and refuses one written); choosing one moves to the same route and search with that `size` and page 1, at once, so the select holds no input, which the generated field facts carry for the kit (see the rules `field-holds-input` and `emitted-modules`): the sources write no `holdsInput` beside `testIdFor(<list>, 'size-select')`.

A part leaves out of the address a value equal to its default (page 1, the action's default size), as every link of the app does. A range or a link that does not apply is absent from the page, never disabled, so a state that lists the list in `meta.renders` does not require them; the size select it does. `pagerOf`, `searchKeysOf` and `pageSearchOf` of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors` compute what a page shows, which search keys a list reads for and the search a part moves to, for the app and the tests alike (`PAGE` and `SIZE` name the search parameters, `PAGING_PARTS` the four parts).

`contract:check` holds a paged source: its action pages, with its sizes each once, the smallest first, its default one of them, and takes the number parameters `page` and `size`, each of type `number` or `integer` as it writes it or as the value it refers to says (see the rule `declared-values`); every route of its screen declares `page` and `size` as it reads them; no source's `params` names `page` or `size`; a screen pages one list; and a paged list has at most 3 segments. A range and the previous and next links are shown where the read decides, so the e2e run's settled check does not require them to be on the page where their list is rendered; the size select shows wherever the list does (see the rule `settled-state`). `contract:diff` lists `paged` on the line of a list that reads a platform action.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.source.paged`](#screen-anchors-name-source-paged), [`screen.anchors.<name>.derived`](#screen-anchors-name-derived), [`platform.actions.<name>.paged`](#platform-actions-name-paged).

### A value declared once

<a id="rule-declared-values"></a>
A value is a type with its constraints, said once where its policy lives, so a limit no longer lives in a parameter's prose. It is a name of `platform.values` (`email: { type: string, format: email, maxLength: 254, description }`), written in the bounds of a route's flat parameter (`type`, `minLength`, `maxLength`, `pattern`, `minimum`, `maximum`), an `enum` of strings and numbers, `examples` (any list of fake values) and `format`, which a route's search never takes; or a field of the model, `<entity>.<field>`, whose JSON Schema keywords a value has it takes: `type`, `enum` (when every member is a string or a number), `minLength`, `maxLength`, `pattern`, `format` (one a value names), `minimum` and `maximum`, and `readOnly` when the field is `x-perch.readonly`; its other keywords (`exclusiveMinimum`, `exclusiveMaximum`, `multipleOf`) no value has, so a reference does not take them.

From version 6 the platform may declare a default length, `defaults: { maxLength: <n> }`, which each value that names no limit takes, a value of `platform.values`, a field of the model or a parameter of a platform action that refers to no `value` and writes a constraint (a `minLength`, a `pattern`; one that writes none is refused, and the default does not stand for it), with `type: string`, no `enum` and not read-only, where the files load and before anything is derived from them (`withDefaultLengths`), so every reader takes the resolved value and none applies the default itself: what a parameter and a field take, a field's `<field>-too-long`, the keywords beside `value`, the manifest, which carries `platform.defaults` as `contract.defaults`. A value that writes its own `maxLength` keeps it, and `maxLength: null` on a value of `platform.values` says it has no limit and takes none; the loader refuses from version 6 a written `maxLength` equal to the default on a value that would take it, and a `null` on one that would take none (no `defaults`, no string, or an `enum`); a route's search parameter never takes it.

A parameter of a platform action writes its `type` or the `value` it refers to, `value: <name>` or `value: <entity>.<field>`, and may write the keywords of a value beside it. A keyword written beside `value` only narrows the value: a `minLength` or a `minimum` no smaller than the value's, a `maxLength` or a `maximum` no larger, an `enum` among the value's members, a `type` or a `format` where the value has none or the same, a `pattern` where the value has none (one beside a value's own pattern is refused, the same one included); `contract:check` refuses one that would widen it or say other than it says, at the keyword (`{ value: email, maxLength: 500 }` over an email of at most 254 characters). What a parameter takes (`parameterValueOf`) is the value it refers to narrowed by those keywords (`{ value: email, maxLength: 100 }` takes at most 100 characters; a refused keyword is left out, so the value keeps its own); a paged action's `size` takes the action's sizes as its `enum`; a parameter that refers to nothing takes its own keywords, its `type` alone at least. A parameter of an action of the model takes the value of the field it sets. `optional: true` says the action runs without the parameter.

The value a field takes (`fieldValueOf`) is the loosest of the values of its model binding and of every action parameter that takes it: its own action bindings and every binding of another anchor of its screen that passes it, a submit button's derived params included. So a field one form uses for two parameters (the password of a sign-in and the new one of a sign-up) accepts what either accepts, never only what the strictest does. A confirmation, an input that writes `confirms`, takes the value of the input it confirms (see the rule `password-pair`). Loosest: `minLength` and `minimum` the smallest, `maxLength` and `maximum` the largest, each kept only when every value has one; `type`, `pattern`, `format` and `enum` kept only when every value has the same; `readOnly` only when every value has it. A `{ search }` binding adds nothing, neither narrowing nor widening: the limit of a search parameter belongs to the address. An anchor that is no field, and a field with no model binding and no parameter that takes it, has no value. The manifest carries it on each `input` and `textarea` that has one, as `ids.<anchor>.value`.

`contract:check` refuses a `value` that names neither a value of `platform.values` nor a field of the model, at the parameter, and a keyword beside it that does not narrow it, and holds the description of each value to the budget; a paged action's `page` and `size` are numbers by the type they write or take from their value. `contract:diff` lists the default length as `defaults added: maxLength <n>`, the values added and removed by name, and of each value both sides hold, as it loads, each keyword whose value changes, compared as data, as `platform values changed: <name> <keyword> <before> -> <after>`, each side its JSON (`none` on the side that lacks it: `kind enum [1] -> ["1"]`); the line of each parameter of an action names its type or its value and its constraints, each as JSON (see the rule `contract-diff-lines`).

Keys it concerns: [`platform.defaults`](#platform-defaults), [`platform.values`](#platform-values), [`platform.actions.<name>.params.<name>`](#platform-actions-name-params-name), [`platform.actions.<name>.params.<name>.value`](#platform-actions-name-params-name-value), [`manifest.ids.<name>.value`](#manifest-ids-name-value), [`platform.defaults.maxLength`](#platform-defaults-maxlength), [`manifest.contract.defaults`](#manifest-contract-defaults), [`platform.values.<name>.maxLength`](#platform-values-name-maxlength).

### A platform code on the parameter it concerns

<a id="rule-attributed-codes"></a>
From version 3 a code of a platform action concerns the parameters it is attributed to (`attributedCodes(action, platform)`, per parameter its codes in the action's order): the one it is named after, `<parameter>-<suffix>` with the parameter in kebab-case (`currentPassword` gives `current-password-rejected`), and each one whose `codes` list it (`codes: [ link-invalid ]` on a `token`); a code the action does not declare is never attributed. The suffixes are those of a field's codes (`FIELD_CODE_SUFFIXES`), each naming a constraint of the value: `required` a `minLength` of 1 or more, `too-short` a `minLength` above 1, `too-long` a `maxLength`, `invalid` a `pattern`, a `format` or an `enum`, `taken` none (any value may be taken), `rejected` any constraint. An error then derives the field a code is shown under from the control passed for its parameter, as it does for a code of the model (see the rule `error-codes-and-fields`). Before version 3 a platform code concerns no parameter.

`contract:check` holds a contract from version 3 to four rules (`attributionProblems`): a parameter whose value is a string refers to a value or writes a constraint beside its `type`, at the parameter; a code that ends like the code of a field concerns a parameter, at the code; a code named after a parameter names a constraint the parameter's value has (see the rule `declared-values`), as the row of its suffix reads it, at the code; and the `codes` of a parameter are codes of its action, at the code.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.actions.<name>.codes`](#platform-actions-name-codes), [`platform.actions.<name>.params.<name>.codes`](#platform-actions-name-params-name-codes).

### The browser refuses an email first

<a id="rule-email-format"></a>
`format: email`, the format every value of a string keeps, an address of mail: the field is `type=email`, which the kit takes from the field facts (see the rule `emitted-modules`) and a view never writes; an editable one is submitted through a form without `noValidate`, so the browser refuses a text that is no email when the form submits, before the action runs; where the action declares an `-invalid` code, it answers what passes the browser, which takes `a@b` for an email. A form that holds such a field never sets `noValidate`, which would let every text through to the action.

A control that does not submit its form (a `type=button` that runs its action on click) runs the action with no browser check: the same `-invalid` code, where the action declares one, answers a text that is no email there. A read-only field of that format is `type=email` too and is submitted by no form.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.values.<name>.format`](#platform-values-name-format), [`manifest.ids.<name>.value.format`](#manifest-ids-name-value-format).

### What an action answers: its outcomes, its codes and the invariants

<a id="rule-action-outcomes"></a>
A platform action says what it answers in two places. Its `result` is what holds once it runs: one outcome, or a list of outcome lines, the plain success first, then each other outcome under its own condition (`with the second factor on: asks for a code and signs no device in`). Its `codes` map each code a failure shows to when it answers: a condition, as the server judges it when the request is admitted (`invalid-code: the code is wrong or used up`), or the invariants it refuses for, rules several actions keep, each said once under `platform.invariants`: `{ invariant: <name> }`, or `{ invariant: [<name>, <name>] }` for two or more, each once, with `condition: <text>` beside them for a cause of the code that is no invariant (`rate-limited: { invariant: [failed-sign-ins, address-mails], condition: a fourth change by the account within a quarter of an hour }`). Each outcome line, a `result` of one string included, each condition and each rule keeps 200 characters, the budget of a description (`behaviourTextsProblems`). A contract before version 6 may write the older forms, `codes` as a list of their names, which says no condition, and a longer `result` of one string; from version 6 the check refuses both (`actionFormsProblems`, `behaviourTextsProblems`).

An invariant, `platform.invariants.<name>`, writes its `rule` and the code it `refuses` with. A rule that states a number (a limit, a window) declares it under `values`, by a camelCase name, as a count (`limit: 10`) or a duration in one unit (`window: { minutes: 15 }`), and writes it as `{name}` where it says it: `{limit} wrong passwords within {window}`. So a number is said once, and the manifest carries the rule with each value written in (a count as digits, a duration as `15 minutes`) and the values beside it; the engine emits them for code to name (`CONTRACT_INVARIANTS`, see the rule `terms`). An action names it under that code, alone or among others, and nowhere else: the engine derives the actions an invariant limits (`invariantActions`), by name, and the manifest carries them under the invariant as `actions`. `contract:check` holds them (`invariantsProblems`): each invariant an action names exists, and its `refuses` is the code it is named under, both at that code; an invariant no action names is refused, at the invariant; a `{name}` of a rule names one of its values and a brace stands in a `{name}` alone, at the rule, and a value the rule never names, or one code would read by another value's name (a duration `window` is `windowMs`), is refused, at the value. The vocabulary's Codes table keeps what each code means to the user; the rule behind a refusal is its invariant's.

The hand-over diff lists each outcome line as `result: <line>`, by its position when there are several (`result #2: <line>`, so a reorder shows), each code as `code <code>: "<condition>"`, `code <code>: invariant <name>`, `code <code>: invariant <name>, <name>`, or, with a condition beside them, `code <code>: invariant <name>; "<condition>"` (the condition quoted, so a text never reads as the invariants a code names), and each invariant as `invariant lines added in <name>` and `invariant lines removed in <name>`, its `rule: <text>` with its values written in, `refuses <code>` and each value as `value <name>: <value>`, so a changed number shows on its own line.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.actions.<name>.result`](#platform-actions-name-result), [`platform.actions.<name>.codes`](#platform-actions-name-codes), [`platform.invariants`](#platform-invariants), [`platform.invariants.<name>`](#platform-invariants-name), [`manifest.platform.invariants.<name>.actions`](#manifest-platform-invariants-name-actions).

### What an action fails with

<a id="rule-failure-codes"></a>
A platform action fails with the codes `platform.yaml` declares for it. An action of the model fails with `forbidden` first when its entity says who may change it (`x-perch.can`), then with the codes of the fields its parameters set, then with `failed`. A field gives, from its JSON Schema: `<field>-required` for a `minLength` of 1, `<field>-too-short` for a larger one, `<field>-too-long` for a `maxLength`, `<field>-invalid` for a `pattern` or a `format`, and `<field>-taken` when the field alone is unique (`x-perch.unique`); a new id and a read-only field give none, and a field's name is kebab-case in a code. So `note.createNote` fails with `forbidden`, `text-required`, `text-too-long` and `failed`, and a `maxLength` added to a field adds its code wherever an action sets the field.

`contract:diff` lists the codes the anchors gain or lose, written or derived, so a constraint added to the model shows on review as the codes it adds.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes), [`screen.anchors.<name>.fields`](#screen-anchors-name-fields).

### A button's params

<a id="rule-button-params"></a>
A `button` whose binding names an action gets, for that action, every param the fields of its part (`<part>.<name>`: an input, a textarea, a select, a checkbox, a radio or a switch) pass in their own bindings of it. The email and password inputs of `user.sign-in` pass `email` and `password` to `platform.sign-in`, so `user.sign-in.submit-button` binds it as `action: platform.sign-in` alone. A param the button writes wins, key by key, so a value no field of its part passes stays written: its binding of `platform.sign-up` keeps `locale: app-shell.locale-select`, an anchor of the frame. Two fields that pass one param with different values are a problem of the button until it writes that param.
