Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.binding`](#screen-anchors-name-binding), [`screen.anchors.<name>.binding.params`](#screen-anchors-name-binding-params).

### An error's codes and fields

<a id="rule-error-codes-and-fields"></a>
An element's `codes` are the `data-code` values it carries, on a `message`, `error`, `toast` or `dialog` (a dialog's code names what it asks, `unsent-changes`, since every dialog looks alike): each a code of the vocabulary's Codes table, which holds its meaning, or a toast code the loader derived. Tests and agents assert the code, never the wording; a domain code (`profile:FORBIDDEN`) is mapped to a contract code (`forbidden`) in the presentation layer and never reaches `data-code`.

An error `<part>.error` that writes no `codes` carries the codes of three sources, each once, `failed` last: the failures of the actions the anchors of its part bind (`<part>.<name>` beside `<part>.error`, in the order they are declared); then the failures of the actions the flow invokes and shows on it, by a written `onError` in any form or by the request pattern (see the rule `request-outcome`), a region written `by` a button included; then the codes the flow shows on it explicitly, `show: { <error>: [codes] }` in an `entry`, an `exit` or a transition. What the file writes wins. So `user.reset-password.error` may leave its codes out: the failures of `platform.reset-password`, which the anchors beside it bind, then `passwords-differ`, which its flow shows on it, then `failed`. On an `error`, `failed` shows for a failure whose code it does not list, and for one with no code.

Its `fields` give each code the field of the screen it concerns, which the error shows under and marks invalid, or `form`, the form as a whole, which is no anchor; a code the resolved map leaves out concerns the form, and an error may leave both its `codes` and its `fields` out. Every other code shows beside the controls of its part (see the rule `error-placement`). A code that concerns a parameter goes to the control its binding or the invoke passes for that parameter (`name-too-long` to `user.profile.name-input`), when that control is a field of the screen: a code a field of the model gives, and from version 3 a code a platform action attributes to a parameter (see the rule `attributed-codes`). Before version 3 a written `fields` replaces the derived one whole, and a code that two sources pass from two controls concerns the form. From version 3 the written entries merge over the derived ones code by code (a code the file leaves out keeps its derived field, and `form` written for a code overrides a derived field), and the loader refuses, at the error, a code it carries that two controls pass and the file does not write (it writes the field it concerns or `form`), and a written entry equal to the derived one (derived; leave it out). The codes and the error fields the anchors gain or lose, written or derived, are listed by `contract:diff` as `<anchor> <code>` and `<error> <code> -> <field>` (`-> form`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes), [`screen.anchors.<name>.fields`](#screen-anchors-name-fields), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.fields`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-fields).

### Whether a field holds input

<a id="rule-field-holds-input"></a>
Every field (`input`, `textarea`, `select`, `checkbox`, `radio`, `switch`) holds input: what is typed into it stays until an action takes it or a `clear` of its flow empties it, so a leave with it typed loses it, and the app is to ask before such a leave (see the rule `leave-questions`). It holds none when its own change applies it at once and nothing else applies it: one of its bindings runs an action that takes the field itself as a parameter, an action no other anchor of its screen runs with it (the language select, the organisation select), or its own event moves its screen's search with it, a `navigate` to a path of its screen whose `search` takes `{ anchor: <the field> }` that no other anchor's event does (a filter applied on change).

A field whose action a submit button also runs with it (the sign-in email), or whose search a search button moves with it (the search box of the accounts), holds input, and so does one whose event moves to another screen carrying itself: that leaves rather than moves the search. A paged list's derived `size-select` holds none by its facet: its choice sets the route's `size` at once. The loader sets `holdsInput` on every field, once the flow is parsed; a written value wins. The manifest carries it, and `contract:diff` lists the fields that start or stop holding input.

In the JSX no field carries `holdsInput`: the kit takes it from the generated field facts, `holdsInput: false` on a field that holds no input and on a paged list's size select, which holds none by its facet (see the rule `emitted-modules`), so the sources check refuses a `holdsInput` a view writes, `{false}` or `{true}`, and nothing is wanted of a field rendered (see the rule `check-sources`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#screen-anchors-name-holdsinput), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-holdsinput).

### Which states a field carries

<a id="rule-field-states-carried"></a>
A field of kind `input`, `textarea` or `select` that holds input carries `data-state` `unsaved` while it holds input no save has taken. One whose binding is a model field (`binding: { model: ... }`) also carries `saved` once a save stored what it shows, for as long as it stays on the screen; a reload or leaving the screen clears it. A field that holds no input (`holdsInput: false`) carries no state. The states come from the vocabulary's "Field states" section, and a vocabulary without it declares none. The manifest lists each field's states under `fieldStates` in `ids`, and the table of them under `contract.fieldStates`. A scenario pair `[field] carries [state:x]` is held to them. The implementation carries `unsaved` on a field while it holds input no save has taken, and `saved` only when its manifest `fieldStates` lists it and a save stored what it shows, while it stays on the screen; otherwise no state. `marksState: false` turns off the colour a field shows for its state; its `fieldStates` still hold.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states), [`screen.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#screen-anchors-name-holdsinput), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate).

### A field that shows no state colour

<a id="rule-state-colour"></a>
`marksState: false` on a field: The field shows no state colour: its border keeps the untouched colour whatever its `data-state`; for a field where fresh input is the point (an entry form). Left out: the field shows it. Only on an `input`, a `textarea` or a `select` that holds input, and never on a form: `contract:check` refuses it elsewhere. The kit takes it from the generated field facts, and a view writes no `marksState` (see the rule `check-sources`). The manifest carries it in `ids` (the app leaves the border uncoloured; the field's `fieldStates` still hold), and `contract:diff` lists the fields that drop their colour as `<anchor> shows no state colour`.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-marksstate).

### Which leaves ask first

<a id="rule-leave-questions"></a>
`platform.yaml` declares under `unsavedInput` every leave that would lose what was typed, and how the app asks first. `question` names the frame's dialog: a reserved dialog whose code names what it asks, its confirm-button lets the leave go on and its cancel-button stays; it runs no action of its own. `screen` (a link, Back, Forward) and `actions` (a control bound to an action listed under `leaving`, one whose run leaves what the user typed behind: a sign-out, a switch of organisation, a change of language) ask through it (`question`) or `never`. `unload` (a reload, closing the tab, an address typed by hand) is the browser's own prompt (`browser`), drawn outside the page, so no anchor or code marks it, or `never`. `state` (a tab, a search parameter inside one screen) is `never` or `question`; `flow` (a navigation a flow makes after its action completed: the action took the input) is always `never`, and `elsewhere` (another tab or device, a database that will not open) always `cannot`. The manifest carries it, and `contract:diff` lists a change of it. `leaving` comes with `unsavedInput`, and the question is a reserved dialog with a code and both answers that runs no action of its own.

A flow's `navigate` is one of these leaves by what moves it: after an invoke's outcome it is `flow`; on a platform or data event, `elsewhere`; on a control's event, to the path of the screen's one binding, `state`; on a control's event, to any other path, `screen`, a leave the user starts as with a link. Such a control asks the frame's question before its event while a change of screen asks, as long as a field it loses holds unsaved input: the fields of its screen that hold input, but those the move carries in its search and the control itself. The loader derives it on the control, `leaves: { dialog, loses }`, which a screen file never writes, and `contract:diff` lists it as `<anchor> leaves losing <field>, <field>`.

For that question to hold wherever the control is acted on, a navigate sits on a transition, never in an entry or an exit; one transition moves once; a control's navigate names a control of the flow's own screen, outside the frame, an anchor it declares or a part one derives, which has no declaration to carry the question and so moves within its screen only; a move to a path of its own screen needs a screen of one binding; and a control that leaves its screen leaves it in every branch of every state that handles it, losing the same fields, since its question comes before its event.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.unsavedInput`](#platform-unsavedinput), [`platform.leaving`](#platform-leaving), [`screen.anchors.<name>.holdsInput`](#screen-anchors-name-holdsinput).

### A composite of the platform

<a id="rule-composite-of-the-platform"></a>
A list with its items that several screens show, where the policy behind it lives in the platform, is declared once in `platform.yaml` under `composites`: its `description`, its `list` (the last segment of the list, ending in `-list`), its `itemState` (a row state each item takes once what it says holds) and its `items`, each by name with its description. A secret input opts in with `composite: <name>` (`password-rules`), and the loader declares on its screen `<screen>.<list>` (kind `list`, the composite's description) and `<screen>.<list>.<item>-item` (kind `item`, the item's description, `states: [ <itemState> ]`), right after the input, each marked `derivedFrom`. The rules a new password keeps, which `user.sign-in`, `user.reset-password` and `user.settings` each show under their new password, in `domain/platform.yaml`:

```yaml
composites:
  password-rules:
    description: The rules a new password keeps, each marked once the password typed keeps it.
    list: password-rule-list
    itemState: met
    items:
      length: 10 to 35 characters.
      digit: At least one digit.
```

The new password of `domain/screens/reset-password.yaml` opts in, and its screen gets `user.reset-password.password-rule-list`, `user.reset-password.password-rule-list.length-item` and `user.reset-password.password-rule-list.digit-item`, none of them written:

```yaml
user.reset-password.password-input:
  kind: input
  secret: true
  composite: password-rules
  binding:
    action: platform.reset-password
    params:
      password: user.reset-password.password-input
```

A composite's anchors are declared anchors the loader derives, not derived parts (`user.settings.password-rule-list.digit-item` is screen `user.settings`, kind `item`); they follow the anchor grammar, and no screen file writes them. The derived list and items are declared anchors like any other: the flow's guards, the scenarios, the tests and the sources name them as they name any. An unknown composite, an opt-in on anything but a secret input, a second opt-in of one composite on a screen, a written anchor whose id a composite derives, two composites deriving one anchor and a `derivedFrom` a screen file writes are problems. The platform's composites and the rows of the vocabulary's "Composites" table name each other, each item state a row state: the table names each composite with what it is for. The manifest lists them in `contract.composites`, the declarations under `platform.composites`, a secret input's `composite` and `derivedFrom` on the anchors a composite derives (`{ composite }` on its list, `{ composite, item }` on an item), and `contract:diff` reports a composite added or removed. A composite declared and used by no screen is no problem: `contract:coverage` notes it (`composite <name> is declared and used by no screen`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.composite`](#screen-anchors-name-composite), [`platform.composites`](#platform-composites), [`platform.composites.<name>`](#platform-composites-name), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.composite`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-composite).

### What an element's kind implies

<a id="rule-kind-parts"></a>
From version 4 an anchor's kind says what a file would only repeat, and the loader derives it: a `select` carries a `data-value` on each choice (`dataValue: true`) and renders its `option`s, a secret `input` renders its `visibility-button`, and a `dialog` and a `confirmation` render their two answers, `confirm-button` and `cancel-button`. Each part is listed in the anchor's `derived`, once, after the suffixes the file writes, so every reader of `derived` and `dataValue` (the checks, the manifest, the hand-over diff, the sources check) takes the anchor as if the file wrote them.

A screen file never writes one of them: `dataValue` on a select, whatever its value, and each of `option`, `visibility-button`, `confirm-button` and `cancel-button` that the kind implies, is a problem at its key (derived from its kind; leave it out). Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes them as before. A paged list derives its own parts by its facet (see the rule `paged-list`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.derived`](#screen-anchors-name-derived), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataValue`](#screen-anchors-name-datavalue).

### The roles of a link

<a id="rule-link-roles"></a>
A link bound to one route (`binding: { route: /me }`) is shown to the devices that route opens. From version 4 the loader gives it the `roles` of the route's binding when the route admits fewer devices than the link's own screen serves (the frame serves every role). A link to a route that admits every device its screen serves, a link of several bindings and a link to a route no screen binds get none.

Roles are read by the devices they admit, never as sets of names: a device holds `guest` alone, or `identity` with any of the other roles, so `[identity, member, admin]` and `[identity]` admit the same devices (`sameAdmission` of `@workspace/ui-contract/anchors`, over the roles the bindings of the screens serve). A screen file never writes the roles the route derives: written roles that admit exactly what the route's do are a problem at the key (derived from the route; leave them out), and roles that admit fewer devices stay as the link's own. Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes them as before. `contract:check` reads the loaded roles as it reads written ones (see the rule `roles`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles).

### The search parameter of a mail's link

<a id="rule-mail-link-search"></a>
A platform action whose `mail` links to a screen names the search parameter that carries the token (`link: { screen: user.verify-email, search: token }`). From version 4 the loader gives every route of that screen that parameter, built from the platform value of the same name (`values.token`): its `type`, `enum`, bounds, `pattern`, `description` and `examples`. A screen file never writes it: a parameter of that name in a route's `search` is a problem at its key (derived from the mail link; leave it out). A mail whose `search` names no value of the platform, or a value a route cannot take (one without a `type`, or with a `format`), is a problem at its link. Before version 4 nothing is derived and a file writes the parameter as before.

The parameter is the screen's key (see the rule `address-key`) when an action of the screen takes it: the first action the screen runs with `{ search: <name> }` gives the first of the `codes` of the parameter that takes it, so the `token` of `user.verify-email` carries `x-perch: { key: link-invalid }`, the first code `platform.verify-email` lists for its `token`. The first action is one the flow's initial state invokes (a parallel initial state through the declared initial state of each of its regions), then one another state invokes, as the file writes the states, then one a list takes as its source: the order the file writes the states in changes nothing before the initial one. A parameter that lists no `codes`, and an action of the model, give no key.

No control of the page sets this parameter, a link carries it: when its platform value has an `enum`, the check wants no control for the values under `x-perch.values`, which the other enums of a route, a page choice, still need.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.search.properties.<name>`](#screen-bindings-search-properties-name), [`platform.actions.<name>.mail.link.search`](#platform-actions-name-mail-link-search).

### Where a mail goes and in which language

<a id="rule-mail-recipients"></a>
The `language` of a `mail` or of `notices`, where the mail's language comes from: a parameter of the action that holds one of the app's languages; `account`: the settings of the account the action acts on once it has them, else the language it signed up in; or, for a `mail` alone, `organisation`: the organisation the action runs in, its default language when its settings name one, else the app's built-in English; or `recipient`: the account whose email the mail goes to, read as `account` is, when an account has that email. A mail's `language` may be a list of these sources, the first that has a language speaking: `[ recipient, organisation ]` is the invited account's language, else the organisation's. Every source but `recipient` and an optional parameter always has one, so it ends a list, and a mail's language ends with one, written alone too (`recipient` or an optional parameter alone is refused).

Their `to`: the parameter of the action whose value the mail or the notice goes to, or `account`: the email of the account the action acts on; for a `mail`, the signed-in account for an action on its own account, else the one its parameters name; for `notices`, as it was before the action changed anything (an email changed is told to the old address).

Keys it concerns: [`platform.actions.<name>.mail.language`](#platform-actions-name-mail-language), [`platform.actions.<name>.mail.to`](#platform-actions-name-mail-to), [`platform.actions.<name>.notices.language`](#platform-actions-name-notices-language), [`platform.actions.<name>.notices.to`](#platform-actions-name-notices-to), [`manifest.platform.actions.<name>.mail.language`](#manifest-platform-actions-name-mail-language), [`manifest.platform.actions.<name>.mail.to`](#manifest-platform-actions-name-mail-to), [`manifest.platform.actions.<name>.notices.language`](#manifest-platform-actions-name-notices-language), [`manifest.platform.actions.<name>.notices.to`](#manifest-platform-actions-name-notices-to).

### A description the engine can say

<a id="rule-engine-descriptions"></a>
An anchor's `description` may be left out where the engine says it: a `heading` names its screen by its title; a `link` whose one binding is a route to a screen's path leads to that screen; a secret `input` holds a secret the user types and never shows; an `error` says why the actions bound beside it, then those its flow invokes against it, did not go through, each of its codes with its meaning from the vocabulary's "Codes" table, the one place a code's meaning lives; and a composite's anchors carry the platform's descriptions. The manifest carries the written description, a secret input's with `(type password)` appended once, else the derived one, and gives each derived part in `contract.derivedParts` the meaning of its suffix. `bun run contract:coverage --descriptions` lists the written descriptions the engine would say itself, for the developer to drop (an anchor a composite derives is never listed).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.description`](#screen-anchors-name-description).

### What a screen's and an element's description say

<a id="rule-description-purposes"></a>
A screen's `description`: What the screen is for: the task it serves and when it is shown, in one or two sentences, so an agent chooses the screen before reading its anchors. Not a list of its parts: each anchor has a description of its own. At most 200 characters.

An anchor's `description`: What the element is for. Its kind, binding, codes, states, roles and the flow say the rest, and what the engine says itself (a heading, a link to a screen, a secret input, an error) is left out. At most 200 characters.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.description`](#screen-description), [`manifest.screens.description`](#manifest-screens-description), [`screen.anchors.<name>.description`](#screen-anchors-name-description), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.description`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-description).

### What stays in a screen

<a id="rule-what-stays"></a>
What a screen would only repeat of the model, the platform and its own bindings, it leaves out, and the loader derives it, the same way every time. What stays in a screen is what a tester or a user can tell apart: the areas and their states, where each control leads, a success or a failure that goes elsewhere than the derived one or shows something else, guards, confirmations and chains of actions. A written value wins over a derived one. `bun run contract:diff <ref>` lists the codes and the error fields the anchors gain or lose, written or derived, so a constraint added to the model shows on review as the codes it adds, and the lines of each screen's flow, its states' addresses, what they render and what each guard by failure code takes among them, and every line of a flow added to or removed from a screen, descriptions aside, so a screen written shorter that loads to the same flow shows no change but the descriptions it stops writing, each listed as `<n> -> gone`.

### A password pair

<a id="rule-password-pair"></a>
A form that sets a new password asks for it twice: the new password, a secret `input` that opts into a composite (`composite: password-rules`), and its confirmation, a secret `input` with no binding, never sent, that names it: `confirms: user.reset-password.password-input`. The two are the screen's password pair, one per screen. The confirmation takes the value of the input it confirms (`fieldValueOf`; see the rule `declared-values`), so it is held to the same limits: where the password's value has a `maxLength`, the confirmation's element carries it too.

The loader derives the guards of the pair, on the parsed screen, each under the name a flow uses (`derive/pairs.ts`), each meaning in a rule of its own:

- `passwordBreaksRules`, `{ composite: breaks }` (see the rule `composite-breaks`);
- `passwordsDiffer`, `{ confirms: differs }` (see the rule `pair-differs`);
- `confirmationStrays`, `{ confirms: strays }` (see the rule `pair-strays`);
- `confirmationFollows`, `{ confirms: follows }` (see the rule `pair-follows`).

Each is added to the flow's `meta.guards` only when a transition of the flow names it, so every guard declared stays used. The checks, the walk in XState, the manifest and an app's machine read it as any guard, by its name, and the app implements it. `contract:diff` lists each as `guard <name>: confirms <differs|strays|follows>` or `guard <name>: composite breaks`.

Refused, each at its key: a `confirms` on anything but a secret input, on an input with a binding, or naming anything but another secret input of its screen with a composite; a second `confirms` on a screen, the first making the pair; and a guard of a pair a screen file writes, `{ confirms }` or `{ composite }`, at every version, since only the loader derives one. From version 4 a screen with a pair never writes one of the four names either: a guard written under one of them beside `confirms` is a problem (derived from the confirms; leave it out). Before version 4 a name the screen writes keeps its written guard, and the others are derived. A screen without a `confirms` writes its guards as sentences, these four names included.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#screen-anchors-name-confirms), [`screen.flow.meta.guards`](#screen-flow-meta-guards), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-confirms).

### The confirmed input breaks a rule

<a id="rule-composite-breaks"></a>
`{ composite: breaks }`, derived for a password pair as the guard `passwordBreaksRules` wherever its flow names it, holds when the confirmed input breaks one of the rules of its composite's list. The composite's `items` are the judge: the input breaks the rules while one of them is not met. The app implements it by its name, as any guard (see the rule `password-pair`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.composite`](#screen-anchors-name-composite), [`manifest.screens.flow.meta.guards.<name>.composite`](#manifest-screens-flow-meta-guards-name-composite).

### The confirmation differs

<a id="rule-pair-differs"></a>
`{ confirms: differs }`, derived for a password pair as the guard `passwordsDiffer` wherever its flow names it, holds when the confirmation is not the same as the confirmed input. The app implements it by its name, as any guard (see the rule `password-pair`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#screen-anchors-name-confirms), [`manifest.screens.flow.meta.guards.<name>.confirms`](#manifest-screens-flow-meta-guards-name-confirms).

### The confirmation strays

<a id="rule-pair-strays"></a>
`{ confirms: strays }`, derived for a password pair as the guard `confirmationStrays` wherever its flow names it, holds when the confirmation is no longer how the confirmed input begins: a character differs, or it is longer. The app implements it by its name, as any guard (see the rule `password-pair`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#screen-anchors-name-confirms), [`manifest.screens.flow.meta.guards.<name>.confirms`](#manifest-screens-flow-meta-guards-name-confirms).

### The confirmation follows

<a id="rule-pair-follows"></a>
`{ confirms: follows }`, derived for a password pair as the guard `confirmationFollows` wherever its flow names it, holds when the confirmation is how the confirmed input begins, all of it or a part, or is empty. The app implements it by its name, as any guard (see the rule `password-pair`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirms`](#screen-anchors-name-confirms), [`manifest.screens.flow.meta.guards.<name>.confirms`](#manifest-screens-flow-meta-guards-name-confirms).

### What a failure shows

<a id="rule-failure-show"></a>
In the `onError` of an invoke, `show: <anchor>` shows one of the codes the failure of the invoked action shows. In an `onError` list with a branch guarded by failure code, a bare `show` on that branch shows the guard's codes, and one on a later branch, unguarded or guarded by a sentence, the failure's codes but those the branches guarded by code before it take; a branch before the first one guarded by code shows the whole failure, and a `show` that names its codes keeps them. So where the state `signing-in` of `user.sign-in` writes `show: user.sign-in.error` on both of its branches, with `emailNotVerified: { codes: [ email-not-verified ] }`, the first shows `email-not-verified` and the second `invalid-credentials`, `rate-limited`, `other-account` and `failed`; a screen may write the codes out instead, and what it writes wins. The error's own codes are not narrowed: it carries every code of the failure, whichever branch shows it. A bare `show` after branches guarded by code that take every code of the failure is refused: that branch names the codes it shows.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on.<name>.guard`](#screen-flow-states-name-on-name-guard), [`screen.flow.meta.guards.<name>.codes`](#screen-flow-meta-guards-name-codes).

### The outcome of a request

<a id="rule-request-outcome"></a>
An invoke whose action fails with codes and that writes no `onError` follows the pattern of a request: entering its state clears the error its failure shows on (unless the state's `entry` clears it already), and a failure goes back the one way it came, showing its codes on that error. The way back is the one sibling state whose transitions on controls enter the invoking state: only a control makes one, so a state a platform event or a data event enters has none. The error is `<part>.error` of the part those controls sit in (of the fields the invoke's `input` names, when no control enters it), else `<screen>.error`; with no part at all, no control entering it and its `input` naming no field (a request a link starts, its input a search parameter), it is `<screen>.error`.

So the state `changing` of `user.settings` may leave out its `entry` and its `onError`: the loader clears `user.settings.password.error` on entry and adds the `onError` back to `kept`, the one state whose `user.settings.password.save-button` enters it, showing the failure of `platform.change-password` on that error. An `onError` written as its target alone (a `description` beside it changes nothing) keeps that target and still gets the show, with no clear on entry; one with actions, or a guarded list (the state `resetting` of `user.reset-password`), is written in full and wins.

A state with no single way back writes its `onError`, and the check names the reason when it does not: one where its flow or its region starts (even when a sibling enters it too), one two states enter, one an invoke's outcome enters, one a platform event or a data event enters, one entered from outside its area. So does a state whose failure finds no error, its target alone written or not. A state with no way back may write its `onError` as its target alone, which still gets the show: a request a link starts where its flow starts writes `onError: <state>`, and its failure shows on `<screen>.error`. An action that fails with no code gets nothing.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.onError`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-onerror), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.onDone`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-ondone), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.entry`](#screen-flow-states-name-entry).

### The region of a button

<a id="rule-button-region"></a>
A button bound to one action that no state of the flow handles gets its region, when the root of the flow has one parallel state. The region is named by the button without its screen and its kind (`organisation.notes.add-button` gives `notes-add`) and comes after the regions written; it counts among the two regions or more of the parallel state, which may write fewer. It rests in `idle`; the button moves it to `running`, which invokes the action with the parameters the binding passes, and both outcomes lead back to `idle`. A failure shows its codes on the error of the button's part, which from version 5 a success clears (see the rule `area-request`); the success of an action of the model says what the controls do by the word its name starts with: `create` (`createNote`), the controls whose values it took are emptied; `update`, they show the stored value; `delete`, the entry leaves its list. A failure with codes and no error in the part is a problem: the screen declares the error, or writes the region by its button with its `onError`.

A screen writes the region by its button, `by: <button>`, when it decides something of it: its `description`, `onDone` and `onError`, and the `toast: false` or `read: false` it says of its action, which the loader puts on the region's invoke; a branch of its `onDone` or `onError` that says `read: false` keeps it. An outcome left out is the derived one; a transition without a target goes back to `idle`. The states of a button's region are the machine's, named by the loader (`idle`, `running`), and a scenario or a test reads them as it reads any state.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow.states.<name>.states.<name>.by`](#screen-flow-states-name-states-name-by).

### The toast of a success

<a id="rule-success-toast"></a>
With a toast anchor on the frame (`app-shell.toast`), every mutation says its success there, with a standard message: each invoke of an action of the model, written or in a derived region, gets on each branch of its `onDone` `show: { app-shell.toast: [<code>] }`, the code being the action's object in kebab case and the past of its verb (`note.createNote` gives `note-created`, `profile.updateBio` gives `bio-updated`), or `<action>-done` for a verb other than create, update and delete; a platform action toasts when `platform.yaml` lists it under `toasts`, with the code listed (`organisations.create: organisation-created`), and an unlisted one never does.

The toast says an operation's outcome once, where it ends: an operation starts with an event that enters a state running such an action and goes on along the branches of its `onDone` that enter another (one Save running `updateName`, then `updateBio`); such a step toasts nothing, and a branch that targets its own state re-enters nothing. An operation of one action says that action's code; one of several says the operation, `<object>-<verb>ed` from the control whose event starts it: the verb is the control's name without its kind, in the past (`save-button` gives `saved`), the object the part the control sits in, or the screen's last segment for a control of the screen itself (`user.profile.save-button` gives `profile-saved`). An operation of several that no control starts (a platform event), or that controls with different codes start, is refused until the screen writes its own message on the toast or bans it; so is one event that starts several such operations at once (the regions of a parallel state it enters, or two regions handling one press with transitions that stay in them), until the screen writes `toast: false` on every action of all but one. One press gives one toast.

The toast stays on unless the screen says otherwise: a branch of the `onDone` that shows its own message on the toast keeps it (a written message wins on its anchor, branch by branch; the other branches still get the standard one), and the one ban is `toast: false` on the invoke, or on a region written `by` its button (the language select's store: the change is visible by itself); a notice or an error written on the outcome says nothing about the toast. `read: false` sits beside it on the same invoke or region, a different statement: the action changes nothing a list of the screen shows, so nothing reads after it; on one branch of the `onDone`, nothing reads after that outcome, and the branch keeps its toast (see the rule `read-after-an-action`). A failure never toasts: it shows on the part's error.

The toast anchor carries every code a flow shows on it; the vocabulary documents the derived ones by one rule row, `<object>-<verb>ed`, the manifest lists each with its action, and `contract:diff` reports a new one. A code a flow writes on the toast itself (a warning of its own), or the frame lists on it, has its own row, even where a derivation gives the same code. The rule row is no code itself: the sources and consumers checks take a toast code the loader derived, never the row, and only the words of a derived toast code are written, in the frame's map.

Keys it concerns: [`platform.toasts`](#platform-toasts), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke.toast`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke-toast).

### The question before a destructive action

<a id="rule-destructive-question"></a>
With a confirmation on the frame (an anchor of kind `confirmation`, `app-shell.confirmation`), every control whose binding names a destructive action asks it before the action runs: an action of the model whose verb is `delete`, an action of the platform whose verb (the first word of its last segment) is `ban`, `delete`, `remove` or `revoke`. The manifest writes it on the control, `asks: { dialog, code }`, the code `<object>-<verb>` for the model (`note.deleteNote` gives `note-delete`) or the platform action's name with `.` as `-` (`organisations.remove` gives `organisations-remove`); the confirmation carries every code asked with, which the vocabulary documents by one rule row, `<object>-<verb>`. Both are derived only: a screen file never writes `asks`, nor the frame the confirmation's codes.

The flow says nothing of it: the press still leads to the state that runs the action, and the app's gate asks in front of the event, its confirm-button letting the event through and its cancel-button dropping it; Enter in a field of the asking control's form asks the same way. The one line a screen writes is `confirm: false` on the control, which runs the action without asking. On a control that runs no destructive action it bans nothing and is refused. Without a confirmation on the frame nothing asks. The frame's confirmation is declared by the frame alone, once, reserved, with both answers and no action of its own.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.confirm`](#screen-anchors-name-confirm), [`manifest.screens.anchors.<name>.asks`](#manifest-screens-anchors-name-asks), [`manifest.ids.<name>.asks`](#manifest-ids-name-asks).

### Scenario files and their ids

<a id="rule-scenario-files"></a>
A scenario is an instruction a person could follow by hand, one Markdown file each, in the e2e tree beside its test:

```text
tests/e2e/scenarios/<entity>/<layer>/[<area>/]<slug>.md
tests/e2e/src/<entity>/<layer>/[<area>/]<slug>.e2e.spec.ts
```

The id is the file's path under `tests/e2e/scenarios/` without `.md`, never written inside the file: `user/base/edit-profile-name`. Its segments go from the general to the specific:

- the entity the scenario checks: `user`, `device`, `organisation`, and `contract` for the contract itself;
- the layer: `base` (the entity works on its own; an area such as `crud` or `list` when it helps), `flows` (several roles or devices meet) or `features` (how one function behaves);
- an optional area, then a kebab-case slug.

No numbers: the tree orders the scenarios and the tags select them. The role is a property of the scenario, in its front matter, never a folder. A path that does not match `^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*(\/[a-z][a-z0-9-]*){2,3}$`, or whose layer is not one of the three, is a problem of its file. The scenarios are sorted by id, an id before the ids that extend it (`user/base/sign-up` before `user/base/sign-up-taken-email`).

Keys it concerns: [`manifest.scenarios.id`](#manifest-scenarios-id).

### The header and the body of a scenario

<a id="rule-scenario-format"></a>
The file opens with a YAML front matter that holds only the structured header:

- `tags`: a list holding `smoke` or `full`;
- `activity`, when the scenario walks one: the id of an activity of `contract/domain/activities/`;
- `devices`: a letter per browser with the role it acts as by the end of the scenario; every device starts as `guest` until a step signs it in;
- `data`: the test data by name, `generate` or a fixed string; `{}` when there is none.

Then the body, with fixed sections in any order: `# <title>`, a line `Where: <text>`, `## Scenario: <sentence>`, `### Steps` with a numbered list and `### Expected result` with a bullet list. An unknown heading, a line outside a section, a missing section or a list item of the wrong shape is a problem, with a line number where the parser knows it (a missing section has none). The whole of `tests/e2e/scenarios/user/base/edit-profile-name.md`:

```markdown
---
tags:
  - smoke
devices:
  A: identity
data:
  New name: generate
---
# Rename yourself on the profile page

Where: [/me] then [user.profile.save-button]

## Scenario: A signed-in user changes their name and keeps it after a reload

### Steps
1. Open [/me] and wait for [app-shell.status] to reach [state:ready]
2. Clear [user.profile.name-input]
3. Enter the new name into [user.profile.name-input]
4. Click [user.profile.save-button]

### Expected result
- After step 4 [app-shell.notice-message] carries [code:profile-saved]
- [user.profile.heading] shows the new name
- Reloading the page keeps the new name
```

A scenario that walks an activity names it in its front matter (`activity: session`), and the coverage note counts, per activity, the scenarios that name it and the transitions they walk. `parseScenario(id, markdown)` reads one into a `Scenario` with `lines`, the line of each key of the front matter (`devices.A`), each section (`where`) and each list item (`steps[0]`), or returns its problems with their lines; `formatScenario(scenario)` writes one, its inverse.

### The brackets of a scenario and what they pair with

<a id="rule-scenario-brackets"></a>
Brackets reference the contract, and the contract check resolves each one: `[<anchor>]` (a declared anchor, or one derived from a declared base with a suffix it lists), `[/path]` (the path of a binding), `[code:<code>]`, `[state:<state>]`, `[value:<value>]` and `[mail:<action>]` (a platform action that declares the mail it sends) and `[notice:<action>.<name>]` (a notice of that name the platform action declares). A value `show` or `hide` is one of the contract once an input renders a `visibility-button`.

In one text (the `Where` line, the scenario sentence, a step or an expected result), a `[code:…]`, a `[state:…]` or a `[value:…]` speaks of the anchor the text names last before it, and the check wants it to be one that anchor carries: a code of its codes, written or derived, a state of its `states`, a value of its choice values, its own or those of an anchor declared under it (`<anchor>.<rest>`: the items of a composite's list, the options of a select, the eye of an input). So `[user.sign-in.mail-message] carries [code:check-your-mail]` holds, and `every item of [user.sign-in.password-rule-list] carries [state:met]` holds through the list's items, while `[user.confirm-email.error] carries [code:welcome]` is a problem, `user.confirm-email.error never carries code welcome`.

A route is read past: in `wait for [app-shell.status] to reach [state:ready] on [/me]` the state is the status's. A `[mail:…]` or a `[notice:…]` ends the pairing until the text names an anchor again, since what follows speaks of the mail or the notice (its language, its link). With no anchor before it in its text, a code, a state or a value need only be one of the contract. Write the anchor first, then its code, its state or its value: a value written before its anchor pairs with an anchor the text named earlier, if any. `Choose [value:dark] in [user.settings.theme-select]` holds the value to the contract alone, and `[user.settings.theme-select] shows [value:dark]` holds it to the select. `referencesIn(text)` reads the bracket references of a text in the order it writes them, `anchorCarries(contract)` says whether an anchor carries a code, a state or a value (one it declares or one an anchor under it declares: codes are the loaded `codes`, derived ones included; states the anchor's `states`; values the rows of its choice values and its options', a pattern row matching, or `show` and `hide` on a `visibility-button`), and `pairingResolver(contract)` says why a value does not pair with its anchor, for the scenarios and the consumers check alike.

The check also holds each device to a role of the contract, the activity a scenario names to exist, a mail to the platform action that declares it and a notice to its action and its name (`unknownNotice`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.codes`](#screen-anchors-name-codes), [`screen.anchors.<name>.states`](#screen-anchors-name-states), [`screen.anchors.<name>.dataValue`](#screen-anchors-name-datavalue).

### The test of a scenario, and the feedback loop

<a id="rule-scenario-tests"></a>
The test pairs with the scenario by its path and names it on its first line, `// scenario: user/base/edit-profile-name`, with one `test.step` per step. `bun run e2e:scaffold <id>` writes a failing test from a new scenario, and `bun run e2e:coverage` checks the pairs, step for step: it runs in `bun run check` and in the pre-commit hook on a commit on `main`, while on a branch the hook leaves it out, so a scenario may wait for its test. The test of the scenario locates by anchors and asserts codes, states and values, never wording.

The scenarios are the contract's feedback loop. A scenario is written from the contract alone: when it needs an anchor, a code, a state, a route or a role the contract lacks, that gap is fixed in the contract files first, never worked around in a test. A gap the contract cannot close in the change is signalled, never emulated.

### The three contract checks

<a id="rule-checks"></a>
`bun run check` and the pre-commit hook run three contract checks. Each prints `file:line: message` per problem and exits 1, or a one-line summary. A commit on `main`, and `bun run check`, which checks the tip of a branch before it lands, also check that the contract is at the version the engine reads (`contract:check --current`). `bun run contract:check`: the files on their own and against each other (the rules `check-files`, `check-bindings`, `check-flows`, `check-platform` and `check-access`). `bun run contract:check:sources`: the JSX of `apps/web/src` and `packages/ui/src` against the contract (the rule `check-sources`). `bun run contract:check:consumers`: the e2e tests under `tests/e2e/src` against the contract (the rule `check-consumers`).

Beside them, `bun run e2e:coverage` pairs every scenario with its test, step for step: in `bun run check` and in the pre-commit hook on a commit on `main`, while on a branch the hook leaves it out, so a scenario may wait for its test. `bun run contract:coverage:check` runs where it runs and holds the block of `COVERAGE.md` to what `contract:coverage --write` writes. The web app's tests hold the module routes and the real router to the bindings, and XState and the app's machines to the flows. `checkContract(root)` loads the contract files, the activities and the scenarios (stopping at their own problems), then runs the cross-checks, `schemaDrift`, `hintBudgetProblems` and `referenceDrift`, sorted by file and line; each cross-check is a function of the loaded `Contract`, and the budget of the field hints reads the schemas the engine writes.

`bun run contract:migrate` moves a contract at an older version to the tooling's, step by step (`--write` rewrites the files; `packages/ui-contract/MIGRATIONS.md` says what each version changed); `contract:check` passes an older contract and names it in one line (see the rule `editions-and-migrations`).

### The check of the files and the vocabulary

<a id="rule-check-files"></a>
Each file parses and holds its shape, and each screen file is named by its first binding. Every anchor follows the grammar, starts with its screen's id and is declared once, and is never named like another anchor plus a derived suffix. Every kind, suffix, state, code and choice value is in the vocabulary and every role is the contract's (see the rule `terms`), every derived suffix ends with a kind of it (the kind of the part it marks), and every code of the vocabulary is used; there are no choice values for a `visibility-button`, whose values its suffix defines. The platform's composites and the rows of the vocabulary's "Composites" table name each other, each item state a row state. The version is one the tooling reads (`versionProblems`): at most `CONTRACT_VERSION`, and from version 2 declared in `platform.yaml` alone, the vocabulary repeating it at version 1 only (its `Contract version` line is there and equal at version 1, refused from version 2). The generated schemas equal their Zod sources, and so does the reference (`schemaDrift`, `referenceDrift`); every field hint of those schemas, each `description` keyword the engine writes, keeps the 200 characters of a description (`hintBudgetProblems`): one past it is a problem of its schema file, `<key> ("<its opening>…" at <its JSON pointer>): the field hint is <n> characters, past its budget of 200`, since a hint says what its key is for and what does not go in it and names the rule of the reference that holds the rest; and each link of a guide into the reference, `REFERENCE.md#<slug>` in the files of `GUIDE_FILES` (the folder's guide, the rule, the skill, the manifest's instructions, the package's guide and the vocabulary), names an anchor of it (`referenceLinkProblems`).

`secret` is on a field alone, and a `visibility-button` on a secret input alone; `marksState` (`false`) is on an `input`, a `textarea` or a `select` that holds input alone; `ranked` is on a message of two codes or more alone, each code listed once; a `composite` is on a secret input alone, names a composite of the platform, is opted into once per screen and derives no anchor the file writes or another composite derives, and no file writes `derivedFrom`.

Every description the domain files write, the maps of meanings included (a screen's `states`, the platform's `events`, the sentence guards of a `meta.guards`), says what its element is for and leaves to the structure, another element, the platform, the model, the vocabulary and the flow what they say, as the schema text of its field puts it; each keeps 200 characters at most, whatever it describes (`descriptionsProblems`): one past it is a problem at its line, `<path>: the description of <place> is <n> characters, past its budget of 200; its purpose: "<the purpose of its field>"`, naming the purpose of its field. `DESCRIPTION_PURPOSES` gives, by its `DescriptionKind`, the purpose of each description field in one text (what it is for, what it leaves to others and its budget), which is that field's `.describe()` in the schemas, so it reaches the generated JSON Schemas as an editor's hint, the model's own description taking its text in the shape `loadModel` reads the document with; `DESCRIPTION_BUDGET` is the 200 characters, for every one; `descriptionLength(text)` counts a description in characters. `readDescriptions(root)` reads every description the domain files write, as they write them, never one the engine derives or the loader fills in as it expands a shorter form.

Every activity agrees with the screens it visits (`activitiesProblems`): every state's screen exists and its address is a binding of that screen; every event is a declared anchor (a derived one through its base's `derived`, never a `visibility-button`) of that screen or of the frame; every transition from a state on an anchor to a target is backed by the screens, the flow of the anchor's screen (the frame's, for an anchor of the frame) reaching a `navigate` to the target's path, in the transition's actions, on leaving its state or entering its target (the `exit` and `entry` effects XState runs on a move) or after an invoke's `onDone` or `onError`, or the anchor being a link to that path (`binding: { route: ... }`); the roles are the contract's and every `data` model is a field of the model; `initial` and every target are states; every state is reachable from `initial` and every state that is not final leaves; every guard is declared in `meta.guards` and every declared one is used. `contract:check` builds each activity in XState as written, with the walk of the flows, reaches every state and takes every transition and every guarded branch to its target (`runsProblems`: a machine XState will not build is a problem, never a crash). Every bracket reference of a scenario resolves, each code, state or value one the anchor named last before it in its text carries (see the rule `scenario-brackets`), the activity it names exists, and every device acts as a role of the contract. A problem of an activity or a scenario is at the line of the deepest part of its path the file holds.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.secret`](#screen-anchors-name-secret), [`screen.anchors.<name>.ranked`](#screen-anchors-name-ranked), [`screen.anchors.<name>.marksState`](#screen-anchors-name-marksstate).

### The check of the bindings and the routes

<a id="rule-check-bindings"></a>
Every binding points at a field, an action or a route that exists, or at a search parameter every route of the screen declares, which only a field that holds no secret starts with, and never on another kind than a field (`bindingsProblems`). Each parameter of an action takes a declared anchor (never a `visibility-button`, which holds no input), a constant or a search parameter, an item or a row of its own screen with a `dataId`, of a list, a table or a section, from a control (a button, a link, a tab or a field) in its part (in an invoke's `input`, a state only such controls enter); a parameter two fields of a button's part pass with different values is written by the button. Each param of a platform source is a parameter of its action taking a constant or a search parameter every route of the screen declares, never `page` or `size`.

A paged source's action pages with its sizes each once, the smallest first, its default one of them, and takes the number parameters `page` and `size`; every route of its screen declares `page` and `size` as it reads them; a screen pages one list; and a paged list has at most 3 segments, as its size options add two to its name (`pagingProblems`). Every path parses in the router's syntax (`parseRoutePath`), every example of a route parameter matches that parameter (Ajv's reasons at `bindings[i].params.properties.<name>.examples[j]`), an empty example of a path parameter is a problem, and a `$param` without an example has a value its schema accepts, else `needs an example` at its property (`routesProblems`, `searchProblems`: the enum controls, save the `size` of a screen with a paged list, whose control is the derived `<list>.size-select.option` and which writes no `x-perch`; `retain`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.anchors.<name>.binding`](#screen-anchors-name-binding), [`screen.bindings`](#screen-bindings).

### The check of the flows

<a id="rule-check-flows"></a>
Every flow names states, events (never a `visibility-button`, which is presentation only), guards, actions, and anchors with their codes and bindings that exist (`flowProblems`): a clear's anchor one that carries codes, which then shows none, whatever its kind and screen, else a field of the flow's own screen, which it empties (a secret one included, never a `visibility-button`); a read's anchor a list, section or table of the flow's own screen whose `source` is a platform action, never one with an entity source or none (`flow/reads.ts`); an invoke's `input` taken from no `visibility-button` either; an invoke that says `read: false` reading in none of its outcomes, and a branch that says `read: false` an outcome of an invoke, never the transition of an event, reading nothing itself, under no invoke that says it already; every branch of an invoke's `onDone`, unless the invoke says `read: false`, reading each list, section or table of its own screen with a platform source that the invoke holds (one whose entry its `input` takes, whose source's `params` take a value its `input` takes, or whose source its action's `changes` names; `containersOf`, `flow/held-reads.ts`), or saying `read: false` itself (see the rule `read-after-an-action`).

A navigate's search keys are keys of the binding it moves to, each `{ anchor }` a field of the flow's own screen that holds no secret (never a `visibility-button`), each `{ item }` an entry with a `dataId` of a list, a table or a section of the flow's own screen, taken on the event of a control (`CONTROL_KINDS`) in its part into a key that takes a string (`flow/items.ts`), and `page: 1` beside any key a paged list of the screen it moves to reads (`flow/pages.ts`); an invoke's input that takes an entry is run in a state only such controls enter. Each navigate sits on a transition, one to a transition, a control's on a control of its own screen outside the frame (a derived part within its screen only), to its own path only on a screen of one binding, and leaving its screen, losing the same fields, everywhere its control is handled or nowhere (`flow/leaves.ts`).

Every target resolves as XState does (a state of the root or a deep target `<parallel>.<region>.<state>` from the root, a state of its own region or an id target `#<flow id>.<state>` of its own flow in a region); each event of a parallel state is handled in one area; the flow reaches each of its states and leaves each plain state that is not final; the one transition without a guard comes last. `meta.renders` lists anchors its own screen declares (a derived part's base, never the part), each once and never beside an anchor it is declared under; a plain state of the root or a state of a region renders every anchor it handles that a state lists (a derived part, or an anchor declared under a listed one, through the anchor it is under). A guard by failure code guards only branches of an invoke's `onError`, each of its codes one its action fails with and listed once; and every invoke whose action fails with codes writes its `onError` in full or has what the request pattern needs: the one way back and an error when it writes none, an error when it writes its target alone (see the rule `request-outcome`). `flowNodes(flow)` lists every state in the order of the file as a `FlowNode` (a `RootNode`, `ParallelNode`, `RegionNode` or `RegionStateNode`, with its path of names and its place in the flow); `deepTargetOf`, `idTargetOf`, `rootOf`, `regionStartsOf` and `deepEnteredOf` read the parts of a `DeepTarget` and an `IdTarget`: the state of the root a target lands in, each region at its starting state.

Keys it concerns: [`screen.flow`](#screen-flow), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.invoke`](#screen-flow-states-name-invoke), [`screen.flow.states.<name>.on`](#screen-flow-states-name-on).

### The check of the platform file

<a id="rule-check-platform"></a>
Every action of `leaving` exists and is listed once, every action of `toasts` is an action of the platform (`leavingProblems`, `toastsProblems`), the frame's confirmation is declared by the frame alone, once, reserved, with both answers and no action of its own, and `leaving` comes with `unsavedInput`, whose question is a reserved dialog with a code and both answers that runs no action of its own (a `dialog` and a `confirmation` bring both answers by their kind from version 4; see the rule `kind-parts`), and an action that asks needs an action under `leaving` (`unsavedInputProblems`).

Every mail a platform action declares goes to a parameter of the action or the account, takes its language from one source or the first of a list of sources that has one (a parameter, the account, the organisation of an action that neither a guest nor every signed-in account runs, or, sent to a parameter, its recipient's, never the last; a source after one that always has a language is never reached and refused, as is a last source that may have none, `recipient` or an optional parameter written alone included), and links to a screen whose every route declares the search parameter that carries its token (`mailsProblems`); an action that sends a mail or a notice has no parameter named `account`, and one that sends a mail none named `organisation` or `recipient`. Scenarios name a mail `[mail:<action>]` and a notice `[notice:<action>.<name>]`, and a test holds a mail it reads to what the action declares. Every notice it declares has a name no other notice of the action has, goes and takes its language as a mail does, never its recipient's, and links, when it does, to a screen whose every route needs no parameter: no `$param`, no splat, no required search key (`noticesProblems`). `platformMailSchema` is the mail (`to` and `language`, each a parameter of the action or `ACCOUNT`, the account the action acts on: the signed-in account for an action on its own account, else the one its parameters name, whoever is signed in; the language also `ORGANISATION`, the organisation the action runs in, and `RECIPIENT`, the account whose email the mail goes to, when it has one, or a list of two or more of these sources in order, `languageSources` reading either form as a list; and the `link` with the `screen` it opens and the `search` parameter carrying its token) and `platformNoticeSchema` a notice of the action's `notices`, a mail that only tells, with no token: its kebab-case `name`, `to` and `language` as a mail's, one source, a parameter or `ACCOUNT` (`ORGANISATION` and `RECIPIENT` are a mail's language alone, and on a notice name only a parameter of the action that has one of that name), `to: account` being the account's email as it was before the action changed anything, and an optional plain `link` to a `screen`, never a `search`.

Every action, of the platform or of the model, writes its `changes`, each name of which is a platform action, never the action itself, that a list, a section or a table of some screen takes as its `source`, written once; the schema refuses an action without them, as it refuses one without `codes` (`changesProblems`, a problem per name at its place in the platform file or the model).

Every parameter of a platform action writes its `type` or its `value`, and a `value` names a value of `platform.values` or a field of the model (`valueProblems`, a problem at the parameter; see the rule `declared-values`). From version 3 every string parameter is bounded and every code of a field concerns a parameter whose value has the constraint the code names (`attributionProblems`; see the rule `attributed-codes`).

Every invariant an action names under a code exists and refuses with that code, and every invariant of `platform.invariants` is named by an action, each `{name}` of its rule names one of its `values` and each value is named (`invariantsProblems`); each outcome line, from version 6 a `result` of one string included, each condition of a code and each rule keeps the budget of a description (`behaviourTextsProblems`); from version 6 the `codes` of an action are a map, never a list (`actionFormsProblems`; see the rule `action-outcomes`).

Keys it concerns: [`platform.leaving`](#platform-leaving), [`platform.toasts`](#platform-toasts), [`platform.unsavedInput`](#platform-unsavedinput), [`platform.actions.<name>.changes`](#platform-actions-name-changes).

### The check of who a route serves

<a id="rule-check-access"></a>
No binding's `guard` sends away an account its `roles` name (`authenticated` with `guest`, or `guest` with any other role), an anchor's `roles` are among those its screen's bindings serve (the frame aside), a link to a route whose roles name neither `guest` nor `identity` is shown to no device the route refuses: every restriction above it (its own `roles`, those of each anchor it is declared under, and its screen's, the frame aside) admits a device by the roles it holds together, so one restriction naming only roles the route serves is enough, wherever it sits on the chain, and the binding at `/$` names `guest` and `identity` (`accessProblems`; see the rule `roles`).

Keys it concerns: [`screen.bindings.roles`](#screen-bindings-roles), [`screen.anchors.<name>.roles`](#screen-anchors-name-roles).

### The check of the sources

<a id="rule-check-sources"></a>
`bun run contract:check:sources` holds the JSX of `apps/web/src` and `packages/ui/src` against the contract. `collectFromSource(text, file)` reads one file with oxc-parser and returns its `Collected` anchors (`data-testid`, `testId` and `*TestId` attributes and object keys, a key a `const` of the file states included (`[TEST_ID_PROP]`), `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constants, one holding `testIdFor()` of another included), the suffixes of its `testIdFor()` calls (each with its base when the call writes it as a literal), its codes (`data-code`, and the kebab-case values of `code:` keys and `<NAME>_CODES` maps; a domain code such as `note:TEXT_REQUIRED` is left out, a `data-code` outside the grammar is a problem) and an anchor built at runtime as a problem (`RUNTIME_ANCHOR`, `RUNTIME_SUFFIX`), and one written as a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant its file does not declare, an import, since no check reads the names of another file (`UNDECLARED_ANCHOR`); `scanSources(root)` collects the tracked and untracked sources under `SOURCE_ROOTS` (`apps/web/src`, `packages/ui/src`) except tests, declarations, generated files, fixtures and snapshots.

`compareWithContract(collected, screens, vocabulary)` wants each literal written once, the literals equal to the anchors of the screen files (an anchor no source renders is a problem too), no literal equal to another plus a derived suffix, every `testIdFor()` suffix in the vocabulary and listed in the `derived` of an anchor of the contract (of the base itself, when the call writes it as a literal or as a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant of its file), so a part no anchor declares, a `visibility-button` no input lists, is never rendered, and every code a code of the contract: a row of the vocabulary, or a toast code the loader derived (the rule row `<object>-<verb>ed` is no code itself; `codesOf(vocabulary, screens)`).

What the contract decides for a field, the kit takes from the generated field facts (`FIELD_FACTS`, by the field's anchor; see the rule `emitted-modules`), so a view writes none of it, and the check refuses each one written, at its line, in ahead mode too and whatever its value: a `holdsInput` or a `marksState`, `{false}` or `{true}`, read as a boolean literal, or as a name or a member passed as written (`holdsInput={localFlag}`, `marksState={props.mark}`), on the element that carries a field's anchor as a literal, as `testIdFor()` of two literals, or through a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant its file declares, alone or as the base of a `testIdFor()` (`sources/locals.ts`, which reads a name in the scope that declares it; an entry of a `testIdFor()` with its `base`, as a paged list's size select is built; on an element that carries no anchor the check can read, a wrapper passing its own anchor on, a name or a member is let by, any other value or place is `HOLDS_INPUT_VALUE`, `HOLDS_INPUT_ANCHOR`, `MARKS_STATE_VALUE` or `MARKS_STATE_ANCHOR`, and for `marksState` a wrapper that carries the anchor as `testId` is read too); a `maxLength` on any element that carries an anchor so; a `type` on a declared text field (`TEXT_FIELD_KINDS`, an `input` or a `textarea`), an email's included, while a button's `type="submit"` or the `type` of a part of a field is none of those (`sources/facets.ts`). A prop a spread on the element supplies counts as written there, at the spread, and an anchor it supplies as the element's (`sources/element.ts`): every object the spread may be, one written there, either branch of a conditional, either side of `||` or `??` (a left that is nothing selects the right and adds no branch of its own: before `||` any nothing, `null`, `undefined`, `false` or an absent member, before `??` only a nullish one, since `false ?? x` is `false`, which supplies nothing, as the falsy left of `&&` and a `false` the file writes do (a `0` or a `''` is a value the check does not follow); a left the file cannot follow stays) and the right of `&&` (or that falsy left), the one a constant of the file holds, a destructured name's key, each element of an array the file states for the `const` of a `for…of` head over it, `for (const inputProps of fields)` with `const fields = [inputProps]` (destructured too, where the file can say every element: a spread element, a hole, an element the file cannot follow, a call or a parameter's member, or an array it does not state, as a parameter's, makes the variable unknown as a whole, and so does a member of it that one element leaves unknown, `row.inputProps` where one row holds `props.inputProps`, or of a name derived from it, destructured from it or an alias of it at any remove (`const { inputProps } = row`, `const r = row`), since the loop's body may guard which element reaches which use, and so does an array the file may change: an array held by a name is followed only where every other use of that name in its scope is a plain read, the right of a `for…of`, a spread of it, a pattern it is destructured into, a `typeof` of it in a type, or a member read that is not assigned, updated or deleted and no call of `push`, `unshift`, `splice`, `sort`, `reverse`, `fill` or `copyWithin`, nor of any computed member, whatever its key (`fields[append](x)`; a computed member read, `fields[i]`, is a plain read, one written is not), while the array passed to a function, handed on (to another name, a property, an element, a JSX prop, a `return`) or assigned anew is not, `sources/changing-uses.ts`; neither the key of a `for…in`, nor a loop's `var`, nor the `let` of a `for…of` head, which the loop may reassign, is followed), a member of such an object (through the spreads it writes: a spread that lacks the key lets the scan go on before it, through spreads within spreads as deep as the limit below; a numeric key is the string JavaScript makes of it, `0` is `'0'`; a computed key is the string a `const` of the file declares, `[LABEL_PROP]` with `const LABEL_PROP = 'aria-label'`, read in the scope that binds the name (the innermost block or function around its declaration, a function's own for its parameters, or the file; a loop binds the `let` or `const` of its head in the loop, a `catch` its parameter in the clause, and the cases of a `switch` (in every case and nowhere past it) and a class `static` block a `let` or a `const` written in them, while a function there binds around them; a `var` binds, as JavaScript binds it, in the body of the function around it, a class `static` block or the file, whatever block, case, loop or `catch` it is written in, so a default of a parameter does not see it; a namespace's body is no scope, so its names bind around it, `sources/scopes.ts`, `sources/bindings.ts`; where one scope binds the name twice, the first declaration is read, but the `var`s of one function and its parameters are one variable, which may hold the value each of them gives it, an initializer, a loop's head or the argument, while a bare `var x` keeps the one it has: its objects are followed in each, an anchor or a key is read only where they all state it alike, and its written type is any of the types they write), and none that a `let`, a `var`, a parameter, a destructured name, the variable of a `for…of` or a `for…in` or a `catch` parameter holds, which the file does not state; a key the file does not state, `[props.name]`, or a spread the file cannot follow, `...props.extra` or the call of an `async` helper, may be the key or not, so the member is unknown there and the properties before it stand as more alternatives; a getter or a setter with the key leaves the member unknown), or each one a function of the file returns where the spread calls it (an arrow's expression, each `return` of its own body, a bare `return` or a reachable end supplying nothing: the end of a body is unreachable only when a `return` or a `throw` ends it, in a block or in both arms of an `if` and `else`, and any other statement, a loop, a `switch` or a `try` included, leaves it reachable; a `useCallback` hands its function on, a `useMemo` holds what its function returns; what an `async` function or a generator returns is a Promise or an iterator, no object the file writes, so the call cannot be followed, though the objects its `return`s write are read beside it: an anchor or a prop written there counts, the element is held to it, and the spread is refused; it is never nothing, so a bare `return`, a fall-through or a `null` in it adds no branch), through the spreads those objects write in turn (`sources/spreads.ts`, `sources/alternatives.ts`, `sources/locals.ts`, `sources/reachable-end.ts`). A chain of names, calls and spreads in a row is followed `DEPTH` (8) steps and given up past them (so no cycle runs on): a spread given up so is refused on any element, whatever anchor it reads or none, since that anchor may lie past the limit (`DEEP_SPREAD`): write its props on the element, or build them in fewer steps. A spread the file cannot follow (a parameter, an import, a value built at runtime, an object with a computed key the file does not state, a getter or a setter) is let by on an element passed its anchor, a component's own props passed on, and refused on an element whose anchor the check reads (`UNFOLLOWED_SPREAD`): write its props on the element, or build them in the file. An anchor a spread supplies in every branch the check can follow, the same in each, is the element's even where another branch cannot be followed: the element reads it, its facts are held to it and that spread is refused. On an element that carries no anchor otherwise, the value under a key the file does not state that names an anchor, `{ [prop]: SEARCH_TEST_ID }` (a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant of the file or a `testIdFor()` that reads as one of the anchor grammar), the same for every such value on the element, is its anchor, since the key may be the anchor's: its facts are held to it and the spread is refused; a value that reads as no anchor, `{ [prop]: t('...') }`, and a literal, which may be any id, an ARIA one's, `{ [labelProp]: 'users.search-help' }`, change nothing. Nothing is wanted of a field rendered: a field that holds no input, drops its state colour, has a limit or is an email carries none of them in the JSX, in the full check as in ahead mode. A flag is also refused on an anchor that is not what it concerns, `holdsInput` on an anchor that is not a field, `marksState` on a kind that is not an `input`, a `textarea` or a `select` that holds input (a form, a paged list's size select included), and either on a part a `testIdFor()` derives that is no size select. A paged list's size select, `<list>.size-select` of a declared anchor whose `derived` holds `size-select`, holds no input by its facet and has its entry in the facts, so a `holdsInput` on it is refused as on a declared field (`sources/opt-outs.ts`).

The other facets of a control's element are held to the contract (`sources/facets.ts`): `aria-invalid` and `disabled`, each read as its source text (`Collected.props`) on the element that carries an anchor as a literal, as `testIdFor()` of two literals, or through a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant its file declares (an element passed its anchor is let by), a spread's props included as above, a prop some branch of the spread leaves out counting as missing. Each field an error of its screen names in its `fields` (`form` names none) carries `aria-invalid={concerns === '<key>' ? true : undefined}`, `<key>` the view's own word for that field. A control of `ENABLED_BY_STATE_KINDS` (a button, a tab, a select, a checkbox, a radio or a switch; never a link or a text field) whose anchor is an event of its screen's flow (a declared anchor of the screen, or a part one lists in `derived`, of the kind its suffix ends with, as a dialog's `confirm-button`) is enabled by its machine: it carries `disabled={!props.can(<its event>)}`, one question to its machine with nothing beside it, read from the tree (one negated call of `can` or `props.can` with one argument, however deep that argument's own calls go), asking about its own event as its argument names it, read where the expression is written (`sources/asked-event.ts`): the `type` each object the argument may be ends up with, `{ type: '<anchor>' }` written there or held by a constant, its `type` a literal, `testIdFor()` of two literals, `testIdFor(<NAME>_TEST_ID, '<suffix>')`, or a `<NAME>_TEST_ID` constant that holds one of these, read where the `type` is written and only through a `const` of the file (a name a `let`, a `var` or a parameter binds, an alias or a base behind one included, or a constant the file does not declare, an import, leaves the event unread), through its spreads and conditionals, a later `type` overriding an earlier one, a spread the file cannot follow being the data of an event that writes its own `type` (`{ type: '<anchor>', ...values }`) unless the file declares the spread's type and that type can carry `type` (a member named `type`, not one typed `never`, `type?: never`, which shows nothing either way, a string index signature (a number one, `[slot: number]`, is none) or a key the file cannot read, of a type literal or an interface of the file, the interfaces it extends and the aliases it names, or of one type of a union or an intersection, as in `checkFor(extra: { type?: typeof reset.type })` returning `{ type: '<anchor>', ...extra }`: the spread may replace the type, so the event is unread unless the file states which events the member allows, string literal types, `typeof <NAME>` of a `const` of the file, by its type: its annotation where it has one, read so, and else the type of its value, the string literal or the template without expressions it writes (in parentheses, `as const` or `satisfies`), the type any other assertion gives it (`<value> as <T>` or `<T><value>`, `T` read as an annotation, so `'<anchor>' as '<anchor>' | '<other>'` allows both), where the value it asserts reads as strings `T` allows, a `typeof <NAME>.type` in either read as below (`'<anchor>' as typeof defaults.type`, `defaults.type` being `'<anchor>'`), an assertion saying nothing otherwise (`testIdFor(...) as '<anchor>'`, `'<other>' as '<anchor>'`, a query the check cannot read), or another such `const` it names (a `let`, a `var`, a parameter, an import, or a `const` of any other value, a `testIdFor()` call typed `string` among them, says nothing), `typeof <NAME>.type` of a `const` of the file, read through its annotation where it has one and else by its `as const` value, whose `type` has the type of the value it is written with, read as for `typeof <NAME>` (after a spread it is unread, the spread's members having the spread's type, but for a spread of an object written as its type has it, an object literal or one a `const` the file writes no type for holds, that sets no `type`, which keeps the one before it, `{ type: '<anchor>', ...labels } as const`), or a union of such (`sources/allowed-values.ts`), which join the type the event has before the spread, an optional member being absent, so that the event is read where they are all one event), or of any event where the file declares the spread's type and that type cannot carry `type` (`{ ...submit, ...draft }`, `draft` a parameter, a variable or a name destructured from one, or a member of such a value, `props.draft`, whose written type is a type literal or an interface of the file without a `type` member, read through the interfaces and aliases it extends and names, or a union or an intersection of such types, `undefined` and `null` among them, `sources/declared-type.ts`; an imported, generic, mapped or utility type,
